"WOMEN'S CHAT."

At the Aldershot Review, it was noticed, as at Ken- sington. some time since, that the Queen appeared greatly fatigutd towards the close of the ceremony. That she is becoming more and more unable to bear excite- ment of any sort, is now an indisputable fact, and the most sanguine ameng us can scarcely hope that our beloved Sovereign will much longer be able to come amen'? us, as of yore. On the occasion of the Review, Her Majesty was most b comingly attired and lo3ked an ideal old lady. Tufts of white feathers appeared in her bonnet, and a lovely white shawl was drawn loosely ever be shoulders. A toilette of this description suits the Queen infinitely better than one of all black, to which she has clung se long. Her Majesty still continues to take considerable interest in the Home Farm at Frogmore, and frequently eaoses inquiries to be made as to the state of the crops. With regard to fruit, the Sovereign's crop of cherries is, it appears, rather above the average. There is likely to be also, an adequate supply of plums of all desceiptions, and the pears promise to do well. As each description of fruit ripens, a dish is placed upon the Royal table,and its presence is pointed out to the Queen• By the way, a new fruit—the coral berry—which is shortly to arrive in our markets, is said to be a grea3 improvement upon our cherry. The fruit is produced in California, and on a bush in great abundance. It is enclosed in a burr until it begins to ripen then the burr opens and exposes the berry. In colour it is fiery crimson, hence its name. It possesses, so say those who have tasted it, a delicious flavour, unlike any other fruit known. _ . _

The birth of yet another daughter to the Czar and Czarina, is a huge disappointment to many concerned. A son and heir has been so ardently longed for and it seems just a little hard that each of the three children born to this illustrous pair should have been of the feminine persuasion. Marriage does not seem to have improved the young and lovely Empress of all the Russias. She is growing colder and haughtier as time goes on, and is very unlike the sweet little Princess of Darmstadt days, the pet and darling of the family. The Russian Imperial Family is the richest of all Royal houses. Apart from his salary as ruler, which amounts to two and a half millions yearly, the Czar as head of the house of Romanoffs, owns more than £200,000 per annum. A fourth ot this is paid in allow- ances, to Grand Dukes and Duchesees,but the remainder is at the disposal of the Czar. The Hapsburgs, Royal House of Austria, are very little if any poorer. They own immense landed estates, and have also inherited lar:e privat3 fortunes. The Hohenzollerns possess a private income of nearly a million per annum, and the Italian Royal Family have a private income of £600,000 a year. Our own Royal Family is by far the poorest of the great ruling houses. London has seldom witness d a more splendid gather- ing th,an that which graced the opening of the Interna- tional C,-.nress of Women. It has been said of the seri- ous minded woman that she is invariably a dowdy, far from fascinating creature in appearance, but the " "assemblage in Convocation Hall proves this par- ticular accusation to be undeserved to a degree. It was a serious assembly in every sense of the word, tut the toilettes en evidence were of the smartest description,and the ancient rusty frock, old battered bonnet, and dis- hevelled head, supposed always to accompany the clever, intellectual individual was nowhere to be seen. Lady Marjorie Gordon, the only daughter of the Speaker—Lady Aberdeen—is deeply interested in the Congress, and has proved of the greatest possible use to her mother this year. During her father Vice-Royalty in Canada, Lady Marjorie became quite a leader among Canadian girls and was well known and greatly liked from one end of the Dominion to the other. She is an accomplished tobogganist and skater and a good hockey player. She is also a fair shot, a good swimmer, and a fearless horswoman.

The numerous bazaars that have taken place in Lon- don this season have been truly brilliant functions. Moreover they have proved highly successful from a financial point of view, and this is probably due to the more sensible method of offering useful articles for sale instead of rubbish. Those of us who patronise a bazaar nowadays can venture forth securs in the knowledge that even if we have to pay heavily, we shall at least get things we can make use of. Silver ware, jewellery, glass,china,wearing apparel, pictuies,and even furniture are to be had at the bazaar fashionable. Much has been said—in the way of abuse—from time to time, about the morning wrapper, but like many other things in this world, the wrapper is a blessing if its use is not abused. For instance, in emergencies, and in times of ill-health, when one has to keep one's room, this article of attire is very desirable. Bnt, when it takes, with regularity, the place of a neat morning gown, and is, as is generally the case, accompanied by an untidy head, and down at heal shoes, then truly the wrapper is a most objfctionable garment. A woman should be neatness and cleanliness itself.

Purses and handkerchit fs grow smaller and smaller. Tbe former is attached to a small chain, which passes round the neck and bangs almost to the waist, and the latter is disposed of in divers ways. Some women insist on a miniature pocket, wherein to stow this scrap of cambric and lace, others prefer a little bag, hung by ribbon, or a gold and jewelled chain, from the waist belt, and ethers again dispense with both and stow the handkerchief away in their sleeve. No dressmaker builds a pocket unless compelled to do so, as even the smallest possible ruins the hang of the present skirt. Preference is still given to Swedish gloves for all dress occasions, and almond and biscuit, are much favoured. For riding and driving, and cycling, buckskin and dog skin gauntlet gloves are sold, in large quantities, and for general wear, deep shades of mahogany, nasturtium, golden tan and brown. To those who invest in light coloured gloves, a wooden hand is a necessity. By slipp- ing the gloves on to this, and going over the soiled places with very soft artist's indiarubber, it is possible to keep them presentable a considerable time. There is no need to have a pair of hands, one, provided e; ith an adjustable thumb, is sufficient. Ostrich feathers are always a pretty trimming for a hat but they are anything but inexpensive wear when one has to send them to be cleared frequently. Here is a recipe whereby this end may be accomplished at home Cut small four ounces of white soap, and dissolve in four pints of rather hot water. This solution must be made into a lather, and then the feathers can be put in and clapped softly between the bands for five or six minutes. Next they must be rinsed in hot water, then shaken till dry. Now for the recurling. Take an ivory or metal paper knife, and go over the feather spray, starting from the point and working down. The spray must he stretched out over the knife blade, edge up,and this edge must be smartly though carefully drawn along the upper aide of the spray till it curls up tightly. Repeat the process till the whale feather is curled._

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR Nmixi, Amongst the many favourite combinations In millinery which I have noticed during the past week have been those of a now shade of yellow called old straw and white, silvery grey and lettuce green, sea-foam green and mauve, bright red and deep purple, and fawn and gold. Hats are trimmed of every conceivable flower, except of course the very large ones. I have lately seen a pro- fusion of poppies mixed with cornflower. Roses we have always with us, and the present season is proving that they are by no means likely yet to go out of fashion. Marguerites are extensively worn just now. A good many woodland flowers are also noticeable including the foxglove. In fact I do not think that you will be making a mistake if you wear any kind of flower under the sun. The hate must also be trimmed with any light and airy material. I ought also to say that flowers are being worn underneath the brim. Large-brimmed hats are fashionable.

Bodices are now if anything plainer, bat still very dainty. They are for the most part similar to the skirt in the matter of material and in every case of a very light material. The necessity for this is very apparent in the warmth of the weather we are now enduring. Still, although a trifle warm it is very acceptable. But to return to bodices. I saw one the other day of a very charm- ing material. The material was grey poplin rather full at the junction with the waste band. The front of the garment across the chest and under the throat was of braided silk, the edge lined with lace trimming.

Many of the old fashioned materials are coming back into fashion once more. This is certainly the case with many of the muslins. A Swiss muslin, our grandmothers' favourite and the conventional garb of all heroines of old-fashioned novels, has come to the front again after a long seasoit of neglect, and this year it occupies the first rank among summer materials. Charming gowns of it are to be seen, and not only in white, but in laven- der, violet, pink, and all bright and delicate shades. Valenciennes or thread lace, either white, cream, or black, is the favourite trimming, and wonder- fully complicated arrangements are made with narrow insertions.

Grass lawns seem to have vanished almost un- accountably, and no doubt that you still miss the pretty cr6pons, for nothing has quite taken their place. There are, however, many cotton fabrics with plain surfaces, and they are made up as morn- ing dresses with contrasting bands stitched with white. Almost all the skirts are either corded or tucked. Hair lines and tiny white spots, how- ever, relieve some of the washing fabrics, and tucks do not look well on them. Drill and duck are admirable for boating and tennis, and instead of being only procurable in white, buff, and blue, are made in pink and lemon colour, so that it is possible even to wear a skirt of this strong fabric and a muslin blouse. Many muslin dresses have such long and elaborate skirts that, though worn in the broad light of common day, they look much more suitable for evening or garden party attire.

I saw the other day a very charming tennis dress. It was of fancy printed muslin, with raised spots on the ground, and a rosebud design all over it. At the bottom of the skirt were double frills of muslin, and the bodice opened over a vest of gauged white chiffon. The transparent folded revers are of the muslin, trimmed with insertions and edgings of white lace, and the sleeves, which are of gathered muslin, have soft puff frills of chtflon at the wrist.

Muslin dresses must be flowing—they cannot be made skin-tight. The new skirts have conferred a great blessing upon the world at large; they have abolished the hideous placket-hole, leaving in its stead a stretched surface, whose severe lines are disastrous to many figures. But some dress- makers actually insist upon slipping a placket. hole into this seamless skirt, with what results may batter be imagined than described. Moral— let all your skirts fasten on the side ; it is prettier and more practical. I am afraid most of us do not know how to make good tea. I have heard that in China, tea is prepared in the same cup in which it is drunk. Boiling water is poured into the cup, which con- tains a good pinch of tea. It is at once covered with a saucer and the infusion is regarded as made when the leaves are at the bottom of the cup. The tea should be taken boiling hot, and the Chinese have a way of drinking it without raising the saucer. When one is fatigued a cup of boiling tea strengthens and refreshes more than any. thipg.

Your loving JENrim

THE SHOCKING DEATH OF A WARRINGTON DRIVER.

INQUEST AT NANTWICH WORKUOUSE. FIVE PINTS OF BEER.

Mr. J. C. Bate, Coroner, held an inquest at the Nant- wich Workhouse on Monday, on the body of Richard Gernon, late driver in the employ of Messrs. Maginn Bros., Warrington, and residing at 245, Liverpool-road, Warrington. Mr. H. Hinde was foreman of the jury. Margaret Gernon, wife of the deceased, stated that her husband left home at eight o'clock on Wednesdap morning, and she did not see him again until Wed- nesday night, after be had met with the accident. He told her he had fallen off the van. He was 29 years of age. Matthew Brown, warehouseman, in the employ of Mr. John Maginn, stated he accompanied the deceased with an empty furniture van to fetch a load of furni- ture from Wbitchurch. They left Warrington at ten minutes to six o'clock, and arrived at Minshull Vernon at ten o'clock, when the accident occurred. They drove three horses abreast, and deceased and witness sat on the driver's seat on the top of tho vehicle. The horses came down the hill near Minshull Church at a trot, and when they reached the level road the deceased and witness changed places, the latter taking the reins. A minute or so afterwards the deceased fell on the road. —The Coronor : Why did you take the reins ? Wit- ness : Beeause the deceased was incapable of driving. He bad had some beer.—What caused him to fall ? He had some drink, and I expect that was the reason. I can think of no other reason.—Where did you get the drink? We got it on the road coming.—Was he sober when he left Warrington ? He was right enough as far as I could tell. Witness, continuing, stated that they had a pint of beer each at Stratton. At Acton Bridge they had another pint of beer each, At Weaverham the deceased bad two pinta. and witness a glass of beer. That was only a mile from their previous stopping place. At Over deceased had another pint of beer, and witness another glass. He did not think he was the worse for drink when he got to Over.—The Coroner: When did you think he was in- capable of driving P—Witness : When we were coming down the hill at Church Minshull. He let the horses go and did not seem to care how they went.—The Coroner: Tbe van was empty. Did not you suggest that he should en inside ? Witness : Ne, I did not think that he was that tad. In answer to further questions,witnesa stated that the deceased did not object to his taking the reins, He fell head foremost off the van. He did not think the wheels passed over the deceased. He pulled the van up. and carried the deceased to the side of the road. He called for assistance, and Mr. F. Davies, of Minshull Mills brought a light. The deceased was conveyed to the public house, and a surgeon from Crewe was sent for. The deceased was brought to the Nantwich Work- house on the following morning. In answer to the foreman of the jury witness said he considered that he was capable of driving, although he had drink, until he got to Minshull.—Mr. Daniels: It must have been very evident that a man who had five pints of beer was not all right. Dr. Charles Lawe, of Crewe, stated that he attended the deceased at Church Minshull on Wednesday evening. He was suffering from an extensive scalp wound about six inches long. The skull was stripped for a width of four or five inches. The deceased was not unconscious, but was suffering from shock. He dressed the wounds, and ereered his removal to the Nantwich Workhouse the next morning as early as possible, There was no fracture of the skull or spine, but the shock had rendered the pulse extremely weak. The wounds had evidently been caused by a fall, as they were lacerated. He could not swear the deceased was the worse for drink when he saw him, as brandy had been administered. He gave him the impression that he was intoxicated by his thick utterances.

Nurse Green stated that the deemed was admitted into the infirmary at half-past nine o'clock on Thursday morning, and he died at four o'clock on Friday morning. He had been attended by Dr. Mauro. the surgeon of the house.

Dr. Lowe, in answer to the jury, said that Brown was perfectly sober when be called to see the deceased on Wednesday night. The Coroner said they would not have the slightest trouble in coming to a conclusion as to how the deceased met with his death. It was beyond doubt that he had so much drink that he could not keep his seat on the van, and in falling sustainei the injuries which resulted fatally. There was no blame to be attached to anyone. They could not blame the young man Brown, because he did not get drunk, and even if he had, they could not have attached any b'ame to him. With regard to serv- iug the man with drirk, when he bad sufficient, it was a case for the police to inquire into, and not for the jury. Undoubtedly the police would make inquiries. P.C. Hindley stated that he had made inquiries as to the condition of Brown, and ascertained that he was quite sober at the time of the accident.—A verdict of " Accidental death" was returned.

THE SEAFARERS :1

A MODERN ROMANCE.

BY JOHN BLOUNDELLE-BI7RTON, Author of "Fortune's my Foe," "The Scourge of God," "Across the Salt Seas," "The Clash of Arms," "In the Day of Adversity," "A Gen- tleman Adventurer," "His Own Enemy,* "Denounced," &c.

===== 0002601_18990630_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XXIII. "THE TIGER DID THAT ! "

When the sun began to drop towards where Africa lay, afar-off and invisible, and when, because of the dense foliage which crowned the elopes of the island that rose behind them, that portion where they were was rapidly becoming shaded from its bunting rays, Stephen Charke said that the time had come for him to think of making his tour of the place, or, at least, of accomplishing a part of it. The air, it is true, still resembled that which one feels when they have approached too near to a furnace or, for a further simile, have. descended into the engine-room of a steamer. Yet, now, there would be no danger of sunstroke, and the expedition, such as it was, might very well. be undertaken.

"My idea is," Charke said, "to begin now, this afternoon, by starting to the left and going on along the shore until lam nearly opposite this place on the other side of the island; then I will come back here, crossing it, and to-morrow do the same thing with the other . side. That way there will not be much left unexplored by to-morrow evening. What do you think ? " "I think," Bella answered, "that you are the most unselfish, heroic man I ever knew. Ah! Mr. Charke," she continued, "I know very well why you are doing this, why you are going to make this journey round the island. It is to satisfy me, it is for my sake. If you were alone here you would never do it, but occupy yourself only on thoughts of how to get away, and--" "No," he said, "no. Had I been the only person who got ashore from the wreck I should, as a sailor, as a comrade of the others, have deemed it my duty to make a thorough search through the island ere I,Look steps to quit it. I shall have, as the survivor of the Emperor of the Moon, to make a report—" "I understand," she said quietly. "I understand. Nothing that you can say will make me feel my obligations to you any the less." For Bella Waldron knew as well as Stephen Charke himself knew, that what she had just said was the absolute fact; knew that he was going to undertake this fresh toil—for toil it was, after all he had gone through, after his bruises and buffeting with the waves, and in this terrible torrid-zone heat—for her and on her account alone. Going to undertake it with the desire of easing her heart- and preventing her, in after years, from being able to reproach herself with having left the spot while any chance remained of there being one of those who were dear to her upon it. Also, she was perfectly aware that he did not for one moment believe—alas! how could he, a trained, intelligent seaman, believe ?—that there was any other soul left alive out of all those who had been in the ill-fated vessel when she struck on the rock. And, being thus unable to believe, could she regard him as aught else than that which, a moment before, she had termed him "a most unselfish, heroic man." Before he left her he asked if she would not partake of some of the tinned meats, or sardines, which he had managed to obtain from the upturned hold, but as the girl replied that it was far too hot to eat anything but the wild fruit growing in such profusion all around, and also that she was not hungry, he decided that he would not open one of the tins for himself. He, too, could do very well on what there was to their hands for the trouble of picking, since, here, in this tropical, steaming atmosphere, eating seemed scarcely a necessity of life ; while, if it were, then those glorious bananas whose golden and crimson hues merged so superbly into each other, were amply sufficient. But, also, he had another reason for not opening the preserved meat. The odour of it would—might—arouse the cub's desires, and, once aroused, they would possibly stir the animal almost to frenzy if ungratified. He rose now and prepared to set out, there being still two full hours of daylight left; two full hours ere the sun would be gone and the swift, dark night at once upon all around—the outcome of a dusky veil which the sun appears to fling behind it as it departs, and out of which emerges black obscurity, lit only by the burning silvery constellations. But, ere he went, he asked if she feared to be left alone for so long. "No," she replied, "no. Why should I? What is there to hurt me here? "

"There may be people on the island, all the same," he replied, even if they have not yet dis- covered our presence; although I do not think it likely." "Nor do I. If there were they would by now, in two days, have observed that "—and she pointed towards the wreck—"and have come down to it. We are alone," she concluded with a sigh. "There is no one else here but you and I." And, still again, she sighed. "Yes," he answered, understanding all that her words meant, all the heartbrokenness that they expressed. "Yes, we are alone; there are no others." After which he left her saying that, by dark, or very shortly afterwards, he would be back again. When he had departed, looking round once to wave his hand ere a bend of the shore hid him from her view—she noticing that, true to his sailor's instincts, he went down to the boat and inspected it, and then drew it up a little more on to the white-pebbled beach and made the painter more secure—she went, first, to the rivulet and cooled her face and hands and feet in it, and made some attempt at arranging her hair, using the stream as a mirror. Yet it was little enough that she could do in the way of a toilet, since she had nothing that would serve either as a comb or soap or towel. After which, feeling refreshed, never- theless, by this attempt, she returned to where she had been sitting and gazed out seaward, meditating deeply. "What an end," she murmured, as she did so, while from her eyes the tears flowed freely now—the more so because there was none to observe them— "what an end to all! Gilbert, who was to have been my husband, dead! Gilbert, whom I so loved with my whole heart and soul, dead! and lying beneath that ship. Oh Gilbert, Gilbert, Gilbert! my love, come back to me! And my poor uncle and; aunt, too. Oh God, what a disaster! What a disaster! " And now she wept piteously, so piteously that, if 3 that other man, he who had risked his own chances to save her as well as himself, could have seen her, the sight would have gone far to break his own heart. That other man! Ay! and her thoughts turned to him too. Almost unconsciously she found herself thinking of him, speculating on what his future would be. "He will never marry," she murmured. "I know it. Feel sure of it. It would be the idlest affectation to ignore his sentiments for me. Yet—yet—how sad, how mournful a life must his consequently be; no home to go to, no wife to welcome him, none to make him happy. Poor Mr. Charke. Poor Stephen. Our lots will be similar, we must be friends, always friends." Meanwhile, he was making his way along the beach, which was still shaded from the sun, it becoming, indeed, more and more so as he pro- gressed further round the island, away from the west. And every step which he took served only to confirm him in what he had believed, known from the first—the unlikelihood of there being any other person saved from the wreck: "Surely," he muttered to himself, "if he should have drifted ashore, it would have been here. The current sets this way, also the monsoon blows towards this island. Living or dead he would have come to this neighbourhood if he had come at all. The tiger did that and, doubtless--" He paused at those words, "The tiger did that!" and for a moment it seemed to him that his heart stood still. "The tiger did that! " Ay, but did it Was he wrong in his surmise, wrong in his deduction? Did the cub land here, or hereabouts? Reflecting, recalling the night before, the early dawn, when Bella shrieked to him and awoke him from a half slumber into which he had fallen, he recollected that the beast had sprung forth from the copse of orange and lemon trees that was to the right of the little spot they had been occupying, the spot where lie had waded ashore with the girl in his arms and where, after carrying her up far above the water-line, he had dropped senseless. It had sprung at them from the right side, was coming from the right; and it was not to be believed, was, indeed, beyond all belief, that it could previously have passed across where they were from the other side without having been noticed by one of them.

===== 0002601_18990630_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
especially Dy 13ella wtio was wide awaxe. %Nab coming from the sight; had, doubtless, therefore, got ashore, been cast ashore, to the right. And YA was seeking for signs on, had set out to the left! Had he, therefore; chosen the most unlikely place in which if, by the most remote chance, any human being should have been washed ashore, to discover them. -

"Shall Igo back, he mused," and begin again on the other side? Shall I? " Yet, as he meditated, he reflected that there would be little use in doing so; certainly little use in doing so to-day. An hour had now passed, or nearly so, since he quitted Bella; the sun he knew, since he could no longer see it, was sinking fast—over the whole of the rich, luxurious vegetation that stretched inland there was now the golden hue, the amber light that, in the tropics, follows after the dazzling, blinding, molten brilliancy of the noontide directly the sun is no longer vertically about the globe. Also, there was the odour of the coming night all 'bout him, of that period of the, declining day when, from every fruit and flower that has drooped through the hottest hours of the earlier portions of it, exudes the luscious, sickly scent that travellers know so well.

"No," he said to himself, "no. It would be little use to turn back now. To begin, again to- night! And—and," he murmured, thinking deeply as he did so, "even though he had been cast ashore, he could not now be alive. Blind—unable to see his way—to find any of all the fruit that grows here in such profusion, poor Bampfyld would succumb to starvation, even if the life had not been beaten out of him by the waves." It was of Gilbert Bampfyld alone that he thought, of him alone about whom he speculated, since, of all the others who had been in the Emperor of the Moon, he was the only one whose body could by any possibility have come ashore. They, those others, Pooley and his wife, were in the submerged cabin, no power on earth could have got them out of it; the men in the galley and the forecastle were in as equally bad a plight. Nothing could have saved them, nor even have released their bodies. But, as he thought of Gilbert Bampfyld, so he felt sure that he also must have perished, even though he was not thus below as those others had been. Still musing on all this, but with one other image ever present to his mind—the image of the woman he loved dishevelled and stormbeaten, but always beautiful—the woman of whom now he began to dream once more, to dream of winning for his own in some distant day—he went on along the beach, his eyes glancing everywhere around and near him. Into the wild, tangled vegetation, along the channels of the rivulet's courses, and sometimes fixed on the beach.

Suddenly he stopped, his heart quivering again, beating fast. At his feet there lay a sailor's rough jacket, with, a little farther off, a cap—a common, blue. flannel thing, such as the mercantile sailor buys for a few pence at a Ratcliffe Highway slop-shop. He stopped, glancing at them, not with that agitation which the greatest romancer who ever lived has depicted as clutching at Crusoe's heart when he saw the footmarks, but, instead, with a feeling of astonishment; yet a feeling of astonish- ment which, he told himself, he, a sailor, ought not to experience. "Do not all wrecks," he muttered, "send forth around them countless articles of debris, countless portions of the raffle that encumbers their decks? What more likely than that a sailor's jacket and a cap should have floated ashore ? " Then he stooped, and, feeling them, found they were thoroughly dry, so that they must have been ashore for some hours at least, since even the fierce sun of the now declining dity coul_cl not have dried them in leas time, all soallbd with water as they had been.

CHAPTER XXIV. BEATEN! D EFEATED !

"No," he said to Bella some time later, and when he had returned to her, "no other signs than these. Nothing," while as he spoke he pointed to the jacket and cap which lay at his feet. He had brought them with him after the discovery, thinking that perhaps they might be useful when the time came for them to set out in the quarter boat, as he fully imagined they would have to do, thereby to reach some other island.

"Yet," she whispered, "if they, if this animal, too," indicating Bengalee, who now, what with his being made prisoner by the rope and also by his long fasting, displayed a horrible state of nervous agitation, a state which frightened Bella and rendered even Charke very uneasy, "if they could be thrown ashore, why not others? Mr. Charke, do you think there is any hope ? " "I cannot buoy you up by saying that I do think so," he answered. "Yet be assured of one thing— you will know soon now. To--morrow, at the tirst sign of dawn, I set out to accomplish the inspection of the other half of the island. It is smaller even than I thought; it will not take long." And at daybreak he roused himself to carry out his undertaking, though, even as he rose from the warm, soft sand on which he had lain, he knew, he felt sure, that he was going on a bootless task. Again and again he had told himself, through the night, that, even though Gilbert Bampfyld's body had reached the island, it had never done so with life in it. Yet he would make sure for her sake and for his.

"Heaven bless you," she exclaimed from where she also rose as she saw him do so, and came towards him. "Heaven bless you. You spare. yourself no trouble nor fatigue on my account." "It is best," he answered, though scarcely know- ing what reply to make. "It is best that there should be no chance lost. If—if--" Then he held out his hand to her as he had not done before, and at once, after she had taken it, set out upon the remaining portion of his search. And, for some reason which he. perhaps, could not have explained to himself, he cast no last look back at her in the swift-coming daylight—gave no word of farewell as he had done on the previous afternoon. That daylight brought a little breeze with it which was cool and soft as it came off the ocean that, for some hours, had been free from the burn- ing rays of the sun; and Charke made his way along the beach now, glancing, as he had done during his search of yesterday, everywhere—into every spot wherein, if anyone, anything, had come ashore, it would probably have been cast. But as it had been when he proceeded towards the left, or south, so it was now as he went towards the right, or north end of the island. He found nothing, not even, this time, a rag of clothing or a spar from the ship. He observed, however, amongst other things of which his vigilant eyes took notice, that here the formation of the island was consider- ably different from what it had been on the southern side. There, as he made his way back 'inland to Bella, cutting across korn the eastern to the western shore, he had found the glades and groves almost flat, except for small knolls and little eminences on which, as everywhere else, there grew the long, deep-green grass, the docoa trees and tamarinds, and the flowering shrubs and bushes. But here, upon the side he was now following, all was. very different. Inland, he could perceive that the surface rose until it developed into quite high hills, and that those hills, forming into spurs as they ran down to the water's edge, created a number of little bays or coves, some of them being scarcely more than fifty yards in breadth. Also he perceived that on high, where the crests or summits of these spurs were, their sides were abrupt declivities resembling often the sheer sides of cliffs instead of sloping gradually and being covered by the deep emerald- green, velvety grass. Also they were white as cliffs —as those of Dover!—and, sometimes, as pre- cipitous. Huge masses, too, of fallen, crumbling rock lay tumbled together at their base and in the tiny valleys which they formed between them, giving thereby real signs either of some convulsion which had sometime or another taken place, or of their lack of solidity and insecure composition. "I shall have," Charke thought, "a mountainous, up-and-down kind of return journey if I go back to her inland. Yet it will cut off a good deal of the way and make it easier for me." He found as he progressed, however, that soon, if he wished to continue his inspection of the whole of the coast, he would, in any circumstances, have to continue his walk more or less inland, since now he could observe, by looking about, that the spurs ran quite out into the sea, so that they hid each little bay from its neighbour on either side of it. Consequently, if he wished to inspect the space between each, he would have to mount to their tops and thus peer down into the recesses that they formed. At present, however, there was no necee- sity for him to do this. Still looking, he saw that there were three more hays, or coves, which he could reach by walking between the feet of the spurs and the water, they stopping some yards short of the gentle surf which the morning breeze was raising. "Three," he said, "three. This one where I am now, the first; then two more. And after that I must ascend and gaze down. There will be no getting farther along the bank." So he entered the first cove, finding it as desolate and bare as the others into which he had glanced in his journey ; bare of everything, and with its

===== 0002601_18990630_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
white beam s vole or or au t otner objects man it* own stones Is though it had that morning been swept clean an elear. The second was the same,l except that here, upon its beach, there lay the long -ton shanks of ai anchor with one arm.. and 11114 upon it, the other gone. An anchor that, he kneW at a glance, had never been made in recent days-' that, by its quaint form,must be some centuries old. And, even as he continued his journey, he wondered how it had come there, and if, in long-forgotten and unnumbered years, some toilers of the sea had been flung ashore there, and whether this was all that had been left by time to hint at the story. Then he entered the third, and last, bay or cove which remained passable by the shoreway-s- -the last he would be able to inspect until he ascended to the cliffs above. As he did so he started—knew, felt, that beneatb his bronze and sunburn he had turned white-1 recognised that he was trembling with a faint,! nervous tremor. This cove to which he had penetrated was different from the previous ones; it ran back between the two spurs which formed ite walls until it merged into the wooded, grassy declivity that sloped down from above, and, at the foot of that declivity was more grass, forming s little carpeted ravine and, with growing on it, some of the island trees—orange trees, lemon trees, even bananas. And on the grass there lay a man. Dead or asleep! A man, fair-haired, clad in a white drill suit with brass buttons—they glistened now in the rays of the risen sun—the white uniform of the Royal Navy. A man who was Gilbert Bampfyld. His heart like ice within his breast—all was lost now, every :hope gone that, of late, he had once again begun to cherish!—Stephen Charke advanced to where that man lay, and, approaching noise- lessly, looked down on him. -Looked down and recognised that here was no sign of death or coming death; that he was sleeping peacefully and calmly, that he was rescued for the second time within the last month from a sudden doom. Also, he saw something else—observed that this man had recovered his sight. This he could not doubt. 'Near him was some fruit which he must have gathered recently. And he had pulled dowu. some of the branches of the trees which grew close by and had shedded them of their leaves, upon which he was now lying, they making an easier pallet than the grass alone would have done, while Charke perceived, also, that he had beeui fashioning a sturdy branch of the tamarind into s. stout stick. Doubtless, he had recovered his sighV through the shock of his immersion, when the ship heeled over. Strong, determined, masterful as Stephen Charles had been all through the disasters which had over- whelmed the Emperor of the Moon; brave and stalwart as he had shewn himself when, with none other left to command the doomed ship but him- self, he had hepled to furl and unfurl sails, to steer like any ordinary seaman at the wheel, and to endeavour manfully to hold her up and ward off instant destruction—he was beaten now. Beaten, defeated! Also, he felt suddenly feeble, so feeble that he was forced to sit down by the saved man's side, doing it, so quietly, that the other did not awaken. Beaten, defeated! Ay! and with nothing left of prospect in the future, nor ever any hope.' Nothing! nothing! nothing! What had he hoped? he found himself asking: what, in these last few days? What dreamt of f A home, a wife; perhaps, in the future, children waiting for his return, running to meet him and to beg for stories of the sea, of tempests surmounted, dangers passed. Now, there would never be any home, nor wife, nor children. Nothing! He had loved one woman fondly, madly; the one woman in all the world for him. Until ten minutes ago he had believed that, some day, he would win her. And now it was never to be. His home would be the miserable lodgings which the sailor ashore inhabits; his existence a long series of toiling across the seas in any ship wherein he could find employ- ment, first one then the other, for poor wages and without one gleam of sunshine to cheer him. What a life! And—and it had seemed only an hour ago that all was likely to be so different. She, Bella Waldron— his love—no, not his! never his now—was being drawn towards him, relied on him, trusted on him, and, henceforth, she would need him no more. This other had come back, would come back into her life again and—he would go out of it for ever. God! it was hard. His hand, as he lifted it in his agony and let it fall again, struck against something hard in his pocket. Thrusting it in-.) the pocket, he felt the sailor's knife there which he had found in the hold of the ship, and drew it forth, regarding it. It was a good knife, he found himself reflecting, a good knife. The man who owned it had kept it in excellent order, too, sharp and keen. How he must have railed at losing it. And he, Stephen, had found it! A good knife, long and stout-bladed, well pointed--a knife that would severe the stoutest cable or--! Men had been slain with worse weapons than this. A blow from it over the heart, under the left shoulder, and struck downwards—yes! such blows must be struck downwards, or otherwise they might fail—and a life could easily be taken. Easily—in a. moment. It was a good knife, he thought again. As he opened it and ran his finger along its tapering blade, and observed the thick solid back which that blade possessed, he could not but acknowledge this. The man who had owned it and lost it had paid money for that knife. This was no slop-shop thing bought of a thievish Whitechapel or Houndsditch Jew, who preyed on poor seamen. A good knife! He turned his head, and looked at Gilbert Bampfyld lying there, still sleeping and peacefully; looked at the man whom he had come out to seek for, and —had found! Found as he had never expected to do, never believed it possible he should do. Looked at him, recognising all that his being there meant, all that this third human existence on the island, where formerly there had been but two persons, meant to him; the ruin that it cast upon his hopes. And again he regarded the knife, holding it by the tip, weighing it, balancing it. It was a good knife, one that would strike hard and sure. And, as he so thought, he rose from his seat, went down to where the surf was beating violently now upon the beach, and flung the thing far off into the sea. Then he returned to the sleeping man, and, kneeling by his side, shook his arm gently,7sayingt "Come, Lieutenant Bampfyld. Cc met Wake up, rouse yourself." (To be continued.) NEWS OF THE GAME.

The Manchester" Evening Chronicle "states that a match is likely to be arringed shortly, between Mr. A. llynd, the Lancashire Champion, and Mr. H. Freedman, the 6cottish Champion, for a reasonable) stake.

A meeting of the Executive of the Midland , Counties Draughts League was held on Monday evening, tee 19th instant. Tne Chairman (Mr. H. Bodfish) laid before the meeting the correspond- ence that had passed between the secretaries of the Hanley and Nottingham Draughts Clubs and him- self in reference to the" tie " in the final round of the Midland Counties Championship. It was claimed by the Norte players that, owing to the failure of the Hanley team to fulfil their engagement to play on Whit Monday, at Nottingham, they were entitled to have the match awarded to them. Letters were also read from Mr. Taft (captain), and Mr. Bell (secretary), of the Hanley Club, expressing regret at their failure to get a team to visit Nottingham at such short notice,and leaving the matter entirely in the hands of the Executive. The general feeling was one of regret that it should be found necessary in the first year of the League to have to adjudicate between two teams so evenly matched, and whose meeting in the final tie had resulted in such a splendid contest. However, it was too late to ar- range another match, oven if Nottingham had not claimed the match by default, and eventually, after considerable discussion, the following resolution was adopted " That as Hanley had accepted the Whitsuntide ciate at six days' notice, and had failed to keep their appointment (for the second time), the Nw tingham Draughts Club be declared holders of the "Birmingham Weekly Post" Cup for the season 1898-99; and that the secretary be instructed to forward such resolution to the secretaries of each club."

SINGULAR AFFAIR AT LIVERPOOL.

In a quiet sqnare approached from Duks-street,Liver- pool, there is at pr sent living a family whese affairs have heoome a matter of more than local interest. Flora Inman, a daughter of the house, ha I aroused the atten- tion of people generally content to mind their own busi, .ness. She was a young girl of exeeedingly bright nature, with a lively disposition, a ready toneue, quick in re- partee, and a hearty laugh eager to babble out on any provocation. Miss Inman was knewu and Idred, and when trouble fell on her from an unexpectcd source quick sympathy wet out from all. How would she take the blow? A more sympathetic beart than was suspeetei lay under the gay and laughing exterior. Her friends missed toe old cheeriness, and " Florrie is worry- ing "was the word passed round. Soon it became ap- parent that the" worts'" was bting deep. The girl was sinking. She beaame paler °eery day, and the neighbours who saw her, rememberieg what her old self was, noticed the change with dismay. For over a year she existed apparently on the verge of the grave, and the expectation was that the cutting winds of February would soon banish from sight the pale and haggard face of the suffering girl. But in the teeth of the incle- ment weather there was a sudden change for the better. To the astonishment of all, Florrie was about again. But here the story had better proceed from the inside view.

MISS FLORA INMAN. (From a Photograph bu Mr. Simpson, Liverpool). Acting on instructions, the writer saw the young lady and her mother. The erstwhile snff rer was lively and jolly enough, anl a question of identity had first to be settled. The young lady admitted that she was MiS3 Flora Inman, of 5, Kent-square. Liverpool. " You should have seen her two months ago," Mrs. Inman remarked. "You would not believe it was the same girl." " What was th? matter ?" the reporter asked. "I don't know and the doctors didn't know. She simply got weak and emacisted ; nothing would stay ore her stomach. I tried her with everything we could think of aisd then called in a doctor. He ordered soda and new milk, but that did no good. Then I took her to a specialist, but he didn't do any perceptible good. I said to him, This is slow work." Yes,' he said, ' it's a long, lingering case." But what's the matter ?' I said. He replied, We call it green sickqeas ; it's very common among girls of her class. She hasn't an ounce of good red blood in her body." "Well, she got no better. She had port wine, claret, malt, b,ef tea—everythinz ; but got weaker than ever. At last she took to her bed, and was too weak to help herself at all. Her face was no different from the sheet, and sometimes I had to li.ten to her breathing, to see whether she was alive or not. She looked like a person a few hours from death.

Here the daughter herself put in a graphic word, " I felt as if I was dying ; it is an awful feeling." " Many curious remedies" the mother continued, "were reconamenned by friends. None of them gave the slightest relief. Thee in 'Tit-Bits,' that a gentleman had left in the house, I read about Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for pale people. What I read exactly fitted my daughter's case. It might have been describing her very self. Off I went to the chemist and got a box. She took one of Dr. Williams' pink pills far pale people thee times a day for a week, and even in that short time she looked different. Her eyes got brighter—they had a yellow cast before—and she got stronger. I got another box and gave her two of Dr. Williams' pink pills three times a day, and she got stronger and stronger. Well, she took four boxes alto- gether, and now she's Cure 1, and to think of the money I spent on doctors and their stuff ! The club collector, when he called last month, said he hoped I would net be going up to the affirm, and look at her now! It's nothing but Dr. Williams' pink pills that set her on her feet, and I shall always say so." " I suppose," I nut in here. " you will not object to my publishit:q this ?"—" Na," Mrs. Inman said earnestly ; "it may do pod to some ono whose . daughter nas been Flerrie here, and the more people that know it the bett.r." 4. yen can tell all the world." Miss Inman added, " that if it hadn't been for Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people I should have been gone now." Her mother slipped away for a moment to try to find a photograph at my requestond in her absence I plucked up courage to ask the young lady her age. She was 17.

A VISIT TO PENKETH FRIENDS' SCHOOL. --- LBy "QUILP Penketh Friends', School justly occupies an honoured place amongst the educational in4itutions of Warrington and district. At this echool many of Warrington's most useful sons have spent their boyhood, and the private Friends' School of Mr. Edmondson's at Penketh which preceded it at the beginnine of the century, will ever be remembered as the alace where John Bright, the great orator, was a pupil. Quietly and unostentatiously,education of a thoroughly practical kind combined with sound religious and moral training has been given in the present premises since 1834. It was to this institu- tion that I journeyed awheel a few days ago with the object of gaining furteer information respect- ing it. A straight run along the mein road through Great Sankey as far as Stocks-lane brings you to within a few yards of the school build- ings. It is somewhat difficult at first so deter- mine whether the school is really in Great Sankey or Penketh, but it appears Penkeli has it by a few yards. With little or no pretensions to architec- tural adornment this educational home lies snugly , nestled behind a clump of trees a short distance from the highway. Here amid rural surrc endings which must appeal to all lovers of nature some 80 boys and girls are gathered together and ale being prepared for the great battle of life which they are shortly to seriously enter upon. On approaching the school the sound of piano music met my ears and this led me to the conclu- sion that the objections of the Society of Friends to'musie are not so seriously upheld as was once I i the case. This opinion received verities tion in the fact that music is now taught to pupils as a special subject and is taken up by many of the girls. The headmaster is Mr. Albert Pollard, 8.A., who has occupied the posi- tion for a little over six years. lie is a model schoolmaster, and not only knows but takes a deep interest in every pupil. It was of him that I learned something of the school, but before settling down to talk I made an inspection of the preoaisess in company with Ars. Pollard, who discharges the onerous duties of housekeeper. Accommodation is provided for 50 boys and 30 girls, and that is about the number at present housed in the institution. The visitor is at once struck with the splendid arrangements made for the comfort and benefit of the children. The physical development of the boys and girls is paid special attention to, every facility being afforded for exercise and oatoor amusements. There are two asphalted playgrounds, and an extensive field recently enlarged to eight acres providing accommodation for cricket,hockey,tennis and other popular gomee. A regular series of cricket and football matches, and annual athletic sports serve to maintain among the scholars a high standard of interest in these pursuits. In addition there is an open-air swimming bath which is taken full advantage of by the boys in the sum- mer months. The girls too, freely indulge in vigorous exercise. Cricket and tennis are engaged ia during summer and hockey in winter. Matches are arranged with other schools and last year the hockey team acquitted itself very creditably. In cricket the girls,l learned, are not so proficient and perhaps this is not to be wondered at when one consideres the physical strain necessary to become a good cricketer. The boys are provided with a changing room where their clothes and boots can be dried, and a change of clothing made before and after every game. Near to is a well-equipped gymnasium fitted up and furnished by Mr. Linsley of Warrington, in which both boys and girls receive systematic in- struction. Mention should also be made of the bicycle stables where about a score of boys and girls' machines are housed. Combined runs are oc- casionally made on Saturday evenings. With so many provisions for athleticexercise it may reason- ably be anticipated that the school will at, least produce some good athletes. The children are the picture of health and happiness and in endeavour- ing to secure sound minds in healthy bodies the committee are pursuing an enlightened policy. We have yet to visit the schoolrooms and dormi- tories, and as room after room is cursorily surveyed one cannot help being struck with the cleanliness and brightness of the whole surroundings. Large and well-lighted rooms; heated throughout by hot water pipes and ventilated in the best possible manner, the comfort of the children is assured at all seasons of the year. A museum and a reference library of standard works are placed at the disposal of pupils in the first class, and there are alio two natural histery rooms where the collections of the boys connected with the Field Club are stored. The ' dormitories are under the control of the Matron and excellent arrangements prevail for dealing with clothing. There are also a " matron's room "for , slight eases of illness, and a sanatorium, known as the Friends' School Day Nurseries, a short distance from the school where ixisolation is obtained in , cases of infectious disease. Fortunately the latter 'is seldom made use of. Both Mr. and Mrs. Pollard very willingly gave me particulars as to the education given and the work of the school generally. The school is ealried on by a committee of the Society of Friends of South Lancashire, and although primarily intended for the children of those connec- ted with Friends, others are admitted. Some of the boys,Mr. Pollard informed me,were sons of farmers ' . in Lancashire and Cheshire. The course of studies comprises all the ordinary subjects of a thorough English education. Tne staff includes the head- master, four male assistants, and four female assis- tants, a matron and housekeeper. It was interest- ing to find that the boys and girls were taught to- gether in the four classes into which the school is divided. The senior class is again divided into two sections. the upper section being prepared for the First Class College of Preceptors exeminatiort. Very satisfactory results are obtained by mixed classes, the girls acquitting themselves quite as well as the boys, except perhaps in such subjects as men- suration. Broadly speaking, the aim of the teachers is rather the development of sound character and general intelligence than the mere acquisition of knowledge. The latter is regarded as a means rather than an end. French is the only foreign language taught throughout the school but it is intended to add German at no distant date. Special care is taken with the pronunciation of French, and a number of the more advenced pupils carry on a correspon- dence with bar; in France. Taere is alse an op- tional class for Latin. Mr. F. Hudson of the Widnes Technical School, is in charge of the manual training in the use of wood-working tools, and a special workshop has been provided for this part of the school curri- culum. Boys of a practical tuen of mind anxious to make bo ' ekcases boxes, Sec., in their leisure time are assisted by a skilled cabinet maker who attends the workshop at stated periods. The pupils profitably spend their leisure time in various pursuits which the master and teachers foster. For instance there is a very successful Field Club. The boys connected with it take rambles to various parts in company with the Head Master, and return with flowers and plants or some- times moths and beetles. In this way the boys obtain instruction in botany and entomology,and the specimens obtained are shown at the Annual Exhi- bition. The girls are encouraged to engage in painting and fancy work. The lecture room, which ' is large enough to contain the whole school at once, is used for lessons in chemistry, and occasionally for lantern lectures. Mr. Thompson, of Liverpool, has visited the school on several occasions and shown some of his finest photographic work, whilst others have lectured on their travels, and quite recently Miss Ellen Robinson described her visit to the Peace Conference at the Hague. Mention must be made of the Association Foot- ball Tournament in which the boys take a great in- terest. Captains are appointed and these officious gentlemen choose teams of six-aside. They compete in turn on the "knock-out" principle, and the mem- bers of the winning team have their names in- scribed on a roll of frame or rather on a mount sur- rounding a picture of the school. For this honour many a stern struggle is witnessed on the football field. Ilonours of a far different hind are the ex- hibitions or scholarships of the value of £2O and £l5 respectively, which are now awarded annually on the results of the Christmas examinations. This

is a new feature, and the first scholarship has yet to be won.

In these days when so much is being talked about religious education in schools it is refreshing to know of a school where sound religious instruction is given without enclosing it in some obnoxious creed. The committee of the Society of Friends fully recognise that there is a field for religious teaching but teach nothing of the nature of a creed. All the scholars, whatever denomination they be- long to, invariably attend the Friends' Meeting for worship on Sunday morning. In additions to this, times are set apart on Sunday evening, and one evening in the middle of the week, which afford opportunity for brief addresses on right conduct, with special reference to the needs of school life.

Reverting to the time when John Bright, was a pupil at Mr Edclondson's Private Friends' School, which, bye the bye, was conducted in the same building occupied by the late Mr. G. H. Bolton, of Penketh, Mr. Pollard told me an anecdote which gives an insight into the character of the scholar who afterwards thrilled England with his elo- quence. It appears that for some vexatious reason or other the Master threw a Latin book at the bead of John Might. The bullet missed its billet but John Bright quietly picked up the book and re- turned it to the Master with the words "Thou may want this again." Within a fortnight another term will have con- cluded at the Penketh Friends' School, and the pupils will gather together for the annual meet- ing. At these gatherings, but more especially at the end of the Christmas term, some of the pupils terminate their school course, and it is with a cer- tain amount of sadness that the boys and girls bid farewell to the old school. They scarce know why but their eyes bear witness to " the wild regret of the last good bye."

NO ORDINARY CASE.—Miss Inman'srecovery (reported in another column) seems like a miracle, but it is a miracl + in which all can believe—even the most sceptical because it has a Eel snti fic cause in Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, which, by removing the imp irities in the blood, went at once to the root of the mischief. WARRINGTON CORPORA- TION BILL.

REPORT OF THE SELECT COMIITTEE.

The Veet Committee appointed to consider the War- ringtin Cmporetion Bill have agreed to the following report. That the Bill in some respects gives power relating to police and sanitary regulations in excess of the provi- sions or powers of the general law. Repots from the Secretary of State for the Boma De- partment and from the Local Government Board relative to the Bill were referred to the Committee. The Bill does not give any powers which can be so well obtain el by means of bylaws, made subject to the reerictions of general Acts already existing. It did net assien a period fer the repayment of any loan under the Bill exceeding the term of :sixty years. or any period disproport,ionate to the objects of the loan. Borrowing powers are given for any purpose for which such powers already exist or may be obtained under the general Acts without subjecting the exercise of the powers under the Bill to approval from time to time by the proper Government department. Previsions agreed bFtween the parties have been in- eerted for the protection of the Lendon and North Western Railway Company. but the Committee only consented to such proviFiona beine inserted in the Bill on proof that similar protective clauses already applied to mains and pipes of the Corpora'i_m passing under the railway of the said company. There are no other circomstancee of wisicb, in the opinion of the Committee, it is desirable that the House should be inforined. The Committee have examined the allegations con- tained in the preamble of the Bill. M 3 amended the same, so as to make it consistent with the provisions of the Bill, as passed by the Committee, and found the same as amended to be true, and have gone through the Bill and made amendments thereunto.

_ Sir STAFFORD Non.rucoTE reported from the Select Committee that they had agreed to the following report. viz.:—That it had been proved to the committee that the Corporation of Warringt,m, b ing the promoters of the Bill hal, by their own admission, used the money of the ratepayers in the employment of political agents for the purpese of influencine, t,e elrctors in favour of the promot,on of the 811, rd had entrusted the supervisiGn of the taking of the poll to the persons employed to promote the Bill. The committee had no't been prepared to reject the Bill on this account, seeing that the expense of its pro- motion must in the absence of any infringement of the Borough Foods Act, fall upon the ratepayers, hut they wished nevertbelesA, to record their emphatic die- approval of the employment of such measures in the promotion of the Bill.

COUNTY RATES.

A letter was read from Me Cheshire County Council on the question of the basis of the standard for county rates and enclosing as required a copy of the proposed basis which has been prepared by a Committee under that Act, together with the amount of the assessable value under the Agricultural Rates Act,lB9B, with other particulars. Any objection to the proposed basis, must it was stated, be forwarded to the Clerk of the County Council on or before the 15th July. It was decided to leava the matter in the hands of the Overseers.

AN UNSATISFACTORY FOOTPATH.

In accordance with notice given at the previous meet- ing, Mr. Mclntyre drew attentionito the condition of the footpath from Fairfi,zld-road to Mill Pool and also the passage at the end of toe property in Fairfield road. The passage, he said, was 3ft wide and should be 12ft• The footpath was in a very bad state the whole of the way fram Fairfiel3-road,to Mill Pool, but the part he raferred to more particularly was that part running parallel with the house at the end of Fairfield-road. It was only 3Ft. wide aod it should be 12ft. The surfale was very un- level. . _

Mr. Meson asked if these were matters they cpuld deal with ? The Chairman said there were undertakings with different people that this footpath should be made. In aoswer to a question, the Coairmai said the agree- ment was between the Pike Trustees and Messrs. Green- all, Whitley and Co.. _ _ _

Mr. Whitley said he agreed with Mr. Mclntyre with respect to the condition of the footpath at the gable end of the house, it was in a very bad condition and dangerow to anyone using it. Ttvre was a grid stand- ing no in it aboLt 6 inches. The footpath from there to the Mill Pool was in a dangerous condition. It waa the only footpath in the parish, and he was sorry it was in such a state. Those who had the matter in hand ought to see it was kept in good order. Mr. Clarke said as regards the passage at the end of Fairfield-road there was a signed agroement between the Pike Trustees and Mr. Pemberton. Mr. Pemberton denied it.

Mr. Whitley : You have told us so. Mr. Clarke said it seemed to him to be a matter for the Parochial Commttee with regard to the passage. They had always understood Mr. Per:nix:Am stood in the position of the Pike Trustees. Mr. Mason said it seemel to him that someone had committed a breach of contract against this Council. Mr. Pemberton said they ought to get the agraement before they did anything. After some further discussion Mr. Pemberton prpoosed thst " Their Clerk should write to their late Clerk (Mr. Ellison) to send all plans and documents belonging to this Council at once." Mr. Clarke seconded and the resolution was carried. On the suggestion of Mr. Pemberton the Clerk 47 as also instructed to take ally proceedings required if he found any difficulty in obtaining them.

THE WIDENING OF A FOOTPATH.

The Chairman said he had seen Mr. Totlow about the footpath opposite the claurch and after some conversa- tion he found their only reason for not wishing this foot- path to be put back was the fear the stumps and railings would not bear moving and that some expense would be thrown upon them as owners in consequence. That was their chief objection and they still were wishful the railings should be set back and footpath made proviJpd they bear no expense. If the County Council were will- ing Mr. Tetlow was agreeable. One of the trustees was also present at the conversation and expressed himself as agrenble. Mr. Pemberton said ho did not fee how they could set the fence back. Mr. Clarke proposed the Clerk write to Mr. Linnetl explaining their position in the matter. This was agreed to.

GREAT SANKEY SCHOOL BOARD.

Mr. Thomas Halton presided over the monthly meeting of the above Board, which was held on Tuesday afternoon in the Board room at the Schools Great Sankey. There were also present the Rev. E. S. Jackson, and Messrs. Joseph Wright, Joseph Kit- shaw, Thomas Perks, as well as W. Jeans, jun. (clerk), and G. Clarke (headmaster).—The Clerk re- ported that there was a balance in hand in favour of the Board of £lO4 13s 7d.—The Master presented a report for the month showing that the average attendance had been 262.9, and fees received £2 Ss 81. Thera was a good deal of chicken pox at Sankey Bridges. —The Master reported that one of MM.'s Inspectors 'visited the schools on the 21st June, and expressed himself pleased with the teach- ing of the children. He said they were working well and on sound lines.—The Rev. E. S. Jackson said it, was Walking Day on Friday, and he asked permission for the children to be fed in the school yard, and to make use of the schoolroom if the weather was unfavourable.—The request was heartily complied with. GRANGE BENNETT. --- PROM THE "JERSEY EXPRESS:I One of the prettiest weddings which has neen seen in St. filler's (Jersey) tor some time teok place on Thursday last week,a.t the English Wesleyan Cnapel,Wesley-street. The marriage was between the Rev. J. Grange Bennett, eon of Mr. John Bennett, of Warrington, and Miss Tillie Dupre, younger daughter of Mr. James J. Dupre. of Cape House, Ssmate 3, St. Clement. Leug before the appointed hour the sacred edifice began to flu, and in the seats specially reseived fcr the members of the family and English friends we noticed Mrs. J. J. Dupe, Mr. and Mrs. P. V. Shortly before 11 o'clock the bridegroom, who was at- tended by Dr. James Bennett as best man, and Mr. Wilfred Dupre, Mr. Herbert Jerym and Mr. Len Ben- nett as groomsmen, arrived at the Coapel and took up their position at the foot of the rostrum to await the arrival cf the bride. Punctually to time Miss Dupre entered the sacred edifier, losking exceedingly charming and leaning on the arm of her father, who gave her away: As the wedding party walked up the aisle, Mr. Cooke, who presided at the organ, played the Bridal Chorus, and this wls followed by the school children, under the direction of Mr. G. H. Tory, singing the hymn "The voice Chet brcethed o'er Eden." Cox in a most impressive manner, and at its conclusio i the happy couple ad- journed to the vestry, where the registers wire signed. Grange Bennett left the island at 3 o'clock on Thursdey atternoan by the s.s. Cygne, for Carteret, en route for Paris and Switzerland, where a portion of the honeymoon will be spent, while the remaining days of the Rev. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT FOOTBALL ASSOCIATION.

ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual meeting of this Association was held at the Baths Hotel oa,Thursday last week. Mr. W. J. Rob- son presided and there were also present : Messrs John Lewis (chairman ofthe Ais)eratiol), JF Vi7hit:.le (trea- surer), T P Bradshaw (secret try), r Mir, Andert3n, Harvey and E WhiMatn (members ot the Executive), and oth3rs interested inthe game.

THE FIRST ANNUAL REPORT. The SECRETARY (Mr. T.P.lisinsuaw),read th 3 annual report which was as follows :—" As Secretary of the Warrington and District Football Association, I have much pleasura in submitting the first annual report. In accordance with the minutes of the !act general meeting the Chairman of the Association (Mr. Lewis) and myself attended the meeting in Manchester in connection with the National Junior Football Association. Mr. Lewis was elected a member of the executive to represent this dis- trict but I am sorry to say the matter is unfortunately, not to be proceeded with as the English Association will not consent to its formation. Since our last general meeting we have lost the services of our old friend Ser- geant Healey, and one of our most ardent supporters. This loss was deeply felt, but a worthy substitute was f und in Mr. J. Anderton. As to the financial position of; the club, the small balance in the hands of the treasurer is due to the unsatisfactory state of the weather on the occasion of the final tie. The net ' receipts during the season amounted to £658 only. Last season the association was composed cf 31 clubs 18 of which competed for the challenge cup, the final clubs for the cup'heing Winnins.7,ton Park and Barnton Albions (winners 1896-7), Winnington Park proving the winners of a well contested game by one goal to nil ; the cup for the fifth time finding a resting place in Cheshire. One pleasing fact I have to record is the number of clubs in the out-districts which have joined the Association. This c early shows that our efforts to promote the game are appreciated by the juniors, in the district. You will have no iced the proposed alteration to the rules which if carried (and I have no doubt we shall receive the whole support of the meeting whose interests we are try- ing to serve) will prove beneficial."; On the proposition of Mr. ANDERTON, which was duly seconded, the report was unanimously adopted. THE BALANCE SHEET. The TREASURER (Mr. Whittle) in reading the balance sheet said he was sorry to say that this their first balance sheet, was not as satisfactory as it might have been, but as everyone knew they cluld not help the weather. The year commenced with a balance in hand of £9 151 lid and had closed win a balance in hand of 14s 11. d. On the proposition of the CHAIRMAN seconded by Mr. HARVEY, the balance sheet was adopted. ------- -

ELECTION OF OFFICERS. The election of officers resulted a 3 follows :—Secratary T P Bradshaw (re-elected) ; tun treasurer. J F Whittle (re-elected); hon auditors, E. Whitham (re-electad) and W J Robson. Mr John Evans of Penketh was re-elected chairman of the Asspcittion and in returning thanks expressei his pleasure+ at the way in which the mem- bers of the late executive had attended the meetings and given support to association football in the district. The following were elected on the executive for tbe ensuing year Warrington district, Messrs. Harvey, Robson. Whitham, and Forbes ; Earlestown division, Anderton ; Cheshire division, Cross, and Mair ; S. Helecs division, Roughley. The election of a representa- tive for the Widnes division was left in the hands of the Executive Committee.

Tne Mo. Presidents and Vice-Presidents were elected en bloc

ALTERXTION' TO RULES. An alteratirla in rule 2 of the Association vras made in addition to other alterations in the rules of the Associa- tion and Challenge Cup Competition. Rule 2 now reads :—" That all clubs affiliated t 3 theis Association shall satisfy the Ex-cutive that they aro :properly constituted clubs, and playing football according to the lawn of Vie Association within a radius of 12 miles of the Warrington Town Hall." Votes of thanks concluded the meeting.
