THE EXAMINER,

JUNE 30th 1399.

ON the subject of permitting A Wobbling the electors to select women Parliament. for two municipal offices in the new Loudon boroughs the present Imperial Parliament hardly knows its own mind. Ministers are themselves divided, and so are their followers. Primrose Leaguers who meet noble ladies in council are quite determined they shall not sit in connection with London's new boroughs. Women may canvass for members of Parliament,may assist In managing the League which is sworn to protect the. Crown, the Church, and the Conservative Party. They may do many things, as long as the Conservative cause benefits, but they are not to be councillors and aldermen. We have now had several decisions. At first it was decided in the Commons that they should not be mayors or alder- men, and then the subject was left over until the Report stage. It was then agreed that they should be councillors and aldermen. The House of Lords Often called a revising body, has decided that they shall neither be councillors nor aldermen. The House of Commons is now called upon to express its gratitude to the House of Lords, or to take the contrary view. The Government cannot help anybody very much, and least of all Can it help the women, because the DUKE OF 14EYONSHIRE has confided to us all the great 'Cabinet secret that. apart from the Leaders of the two Houses, LOB.D SALISBURY and Mr. BALFOUR, t.bere is no one in the Cabinet anxious to see them In any further public offices. The advocates who want to see women on the London Councils will make as big a muster as they can, and ,endeavour te procure the insertion of the words which have lieon deleted. Opponents will muster for another big fight, and there will be a great division. If it *.hould end in a victory for the amended clause as it left the House of Commons, there will be much Noicing, though the chance of the Government giving way is not great, and the House of Lords will once more dominate the situation. It is Oil the cards, however, that the Commons will cefuse to make any further change in the tt6 it affects the political status of women, alid. then the two houses will have placed tLbeluselves in agreement. A division of :this Would indicate conviction, and obtain a cer- '4lll amount of respect. But the present Parlia- ment wobbles incessantly, in obedience to many Zntives. It applauded the CHANCELLOR of the 3:cURQuEn when he declared that it was im- 13Ncticable to amend the law about tithe. It 4ePlauded him when he said the advance up the nudan would not cost the British taxpayer a tellnY• It also applauded Mr. LONG when he r,i'cught in the Tithe Bill, and it applauded the ;11ANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER when he came to Q 8 House and asked for the presentation to Egypt, 43 a gift, of the money we had advanced as a loan and were to have been repaid. To keep the present (government in power Parliament it would declare, 'by force of faith, that black is white." It has, really, no convictions about anything, and hence Ye say that the sooner the Government makes up its mind to get rid of it the better. The growing 416covery that the end of a wobbling Parliament tlnal' mean the end of a Ministry emboldens the atter to prolong the existence of the former.

THIS phrase, hurled at the Not a THIS , , new F a Syndieate. rench Ministry, may but be taken to represent the ptiewest version of a selected Cabinet. But in r4L'lce they have destroyed parties, in the old :Ilse, and no party Ministry is, therefore possible. e-he 'Waldeck-Roussoau Government may be des- as a syndicate. It is formed to carry on it business of the country, and to prevent the trench Army from becoming political and exercis- Chg influence over judicial tribunals. Already of: new Government has had to put talking cers under discipline, and the incriminated rnerals who procured the conviction and degra- walstion of CAPTAIN DREYFUS cannot be expected to "1137 escape. The selection of individuals with t f°llowing means a syndicate in the political Tehzlso,and what else is possible in France just now? twe Republic is exposed to danger on of ° sides—from the plots and intrigues discarded parties, Bonapartist, Bon- Legititnist ; and from the utter ,}le. &illness of the common people at so many he"ges in Ministers and policies. We often at it Egid in this country that the French

people make so many changes because they love the excitement, and will have it, whether Ministries fail or institutions go down in the strife. It is true they are excitable, but they do not mean, for all that, to see the Republic go to the ground, and to bring back the Generals who are capable of ordering forgeries to convict an in- nocent man and of using troops to suppress the representatives of the people. A lino must be drawn at the maintenance of the Republic, and it was drawn on Monday night, when the Ministry described by a Boulangist as a syndicate, managed to obtain a vote of confidence in the Senate and the Chamber, and has lived through its first sto-r—m• Stupid it may be, capable it must be, to have sur- vived, and we may see the syndicate governing France in the interests of the people, and not in the interests of section. A syndi- cate that will do this deserves well of the country, and may live longer than a combination which starts with the blare of trumpets and then is intrigued against imme- diately. The present position in France is very peculiar, and yet it may not be dangerous. "What we see is the general impotence of all parties and persons. There are small eddies, but no currents. There are many corporals, but no commanding officers. There is much free thought, but there is no great thought. Some external or internal danger is needed to bring about a shock that would rearrange all these political particles in a few minutes, as a fiddle-bow drawn over the edge of a glass plate of sand immediately disposes the sand into lines and figures. The Waldeck-Rous- seau syndicate is not of a very high order of merit, as Ministries go. It has made a majority, and it has saved the situation. Nothing more was to be expected of it. Yet it my remain intact,acquire authority and strength, and end by being one of the most stable Ministries France has had for years. Who knows? There are always some .wonders coming on in France, and it would not be amazing if a Government which barely escaped defeat on its overture lived long enough to disap- point the prophets of evil.

Frodsham, the most popular holiday resort within twenty miles of Warrington, is infected with an epidemic of suicides and the inhabitants of the township are leading anything but a cheer- ful existence in consequence. Within the last few weeks the village has been thrown into con- sternation on three occasious by the suicide of male residents, the latest—and it hoped the last— of these distressing incidents occurring on Thurs- daylast week. The theory has been often advanced that the suicide of one person acts as an incentive to others instead of a deterrent, and the theory has been often borne out by facts which appear conclusive. It is the same with crimes which are of a particularly malignant type. For instance we all recollect how numerous were the attempts at train wrecking in various parts of the country not many months ago. As there was no suspicion of a system or organised plan in the committal of ithese deeds, it can only be surmised, as in the case of the suicides at Frodsham, that the act of one mad individual stirred up a spirit of emulation in the diseased brains of other people. Fortunately these dastardly attempts at train-wrecking have ceased,and we hope, with the distressed Frodsham people, that their neighbourhood may not be shocked by any further continuation of this sad sequence of suicides.

Next to the third test match now proceeding at. Leeds the most interesting event in the cricket field is the wonderful innings of Master A. E. J. Collins, the fourteen-year-old Clifton College boy who has beaten all records by compiling the huge total of 628 not out. Mr. A. E. Stoddart, the famous Middlesex amateur. has held for thirteen years the honour of being the top scorer in cricket with 485,and now he has to give way to a youngs- ter whom one would expect to know little more about the game than the right side of his bat. Young Collins has become a national hero, and at Clifton College he can hardly be looked upon as less than a God, so prodigious is his performance. What people are wondering is the county for which he will' eventually qualify, and Mr. Troup, the Gloucestershire captain, .is already pictured in cartoons looking with longing eyes on the pre- cocious youth who has dared to surpass the records of the greatest cricketers who have ever lived. Time alone will disclose Master Collins' future,but we may mention that his birth qualifica- tion is for India and not for any English county. We wish him "more power to his bat," to parody a popular saying, and trust that he may justify the predictions his marvellous record has given rise to.

In introducing a Bill to amend the law with res- pect to the payment of rates on tithe rent-charge attached to a benefice the Government has done an infamous thing. It. is true enough that the clergy who rely upon tithes suffer considerably by being rated for local taxation upon their gross in- come, but that is no earthly reason why they should be relieved at the expense of the state. The burden is one which they have borne for cen- turies, and it is a standing disgrace to the Church of England that they should have had to bear it, but the discredit is as no- thing compared with that attached to such a mean action as to seek aid from the tax- payers of the country without their permission. The Church of England is one of the wealthiest bodies in the world, and could very easily support its own clergy if it only possessed a sufficient sense of fairness to do so, but it does not unfortunately ; and a matter for further regret is that we have a Government weak enough or wicked enough to grant assistance at the expense of the whole community. £87,000 would be required to pay the half of the rates from which the clergy are proposed to be relieved by this Bill, and it means practically that the Church of Engltnd is to be further endowed to that extent. It is nothing short of scandalous in its injustice to all other denominations. Sir Henry Cainpbell-Bannerman rightly urged that any necessary relief should be undertaken by the wealthy classes in the Church itself, after the manner of the Established Church of Scotland, which has shown a manly self-reliance in making good the deficient stipends of its clergy. The first reading of the Bill was carried by a majority of 78, but we feel convinced that it will eventually succumb to the opposition which it has aroused all over the country. The public has submitted, with a bad grace, to the doll° fcr the landlords and the clergy interested in de- nominational schools, bit we cannot imagine it sitting down under the infliction of such a bare- faced piece of party "jobbery" as the foregoing.

One cannot help wondering what were the thoughts of the American President-maker, Mr. Hanna, McKinley's runner, as he witnessed that pathetic pretest of Mr. Whiteley against his own party in the House of Commons on Tuesday. Mr. Hanna chanced to come down to the Strangers' Gallery, and it was a new experience to him. He had observed nothing like it in the land of the Almighty Dollar. Here was a member of Parliament, whose opinions on five questions out of six were with the Unionists, separating himself from his friends, going to the expense of another election, and risking a political career, because he objected te three particular bills. To the Ameri- can organiser this transfer of allegiance at a loss to the person making a change, was, no doubt, quite incomprehensible. Mr. Hanna would have understood t if he could have heard a whisper that the Liberal organisation in Parliament-street had arranged this drama. But DO breath of suspicion attached to Mr. Whiteley. His action was to his own detriment. Americans have still something to learn.

A mile a minute on a cycle will soon become an accomplished fact for last week .Ir. C. M. Murphy, a Brooklyn cyclist covered the distance

===== 0002601_18990630_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY JUNE 30, 1899.

in6s seconds at the first attempt He should thus have little difficulty in wiping out the extra five seconds in future trials. But we must explain, for the world's cycle record for the mile stands at 1 min. 35 secs., and Mr. Murphy has given it a big beating. He, however, was paced by a loco- motive and rode on a plank track laid between the railway metals on bong Island. It has long been thought that could a cyclist " hang on "to a locomotive he would put up some remarkable times, and Mr. Murphy has proved the idea to be correct. But is this cycling? Pacemaking nowadays is going too far. We barely tolerated the windshields in Cordang's great 24 hours side, then we had next to accustom ourselves to motor-car pacing, and now the loco- motive has entered the field as a means of drawing the cyclist onwards. We say "drawing" advisedly, for that is what it amounts to. Mr. Murphy cannot propel a cycle at the rate of a mile in 65 seconds unaided, but when he gets within the tremendous suction of a railway engine there is hardly a limit to the speed he—aFid the engine—may attain. We maintain that such records as these are not cycle records. The rider might just as well be hitched on to his mechanical pacer by a rope, when of course, he would get no credit for the speed attained. It is time pacing methods such as these were discountenanced. Let us resort to the old style of man against man, and then our racing idols will be athletes worthy of the admiration they win by their exploits. The other events would then be looked upon in the light they ought to be, viz., remarkably ingenious per- formances, but not cycle races or records.

The number of things which ladies may not do in hotels will soon be as large as the number of things they may do if publicans continue their present course of action. Only a few months ago a lady customer attired in " rationals " was re- fused admission to the coffee-room of an hotel by the somewhat:prudish landlady. The landlord of a London hotel has gone one better, so to speak,for he ejected a lady from his premises, in company with her husband and a friend, merely because she had a liking for the weed, and indulged in it in the bar. Boniface seems to have been legally right, and could probably take similar prohibitive measures with his male customers if he so desired, but that is a very remote possibility. But in what light are we to take these new landlordial regula- tions? Publicans are either becoming auto- cratic, or they are developing into moral reformers on a small scale. As a moral reformer" mins host" appears in a new and strange rOle, and one which he has plenty of op- portunity of acting up to. Whatever the new de- velopment may really mean it is plain that the license-holders have no intention of showing laxity in the good management of their house:. We do not, however, subscribe to their views as demon- strated in the cases msnti3ned.

===== 0002601_18990630_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN NOTES,

LBY A CORRESPONDENT.] Lamentable revelations were made at the inquest held by Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner, at the Runcorn Police Court, last Monday afternoon. A poor woman met an undoubtedly untimely death trough ignorance and neglect, on the part of those in whose care she was. A perusal of the case fills the reader with feelings of downright disgust, and the paper is laid down with the firm conviction that all the coroner said in indignant censure was more than deserved.

"Beer and buttermilk " ! what stuff upon which t 3 attempt to sustain a woman in such a delicate condition. The midwife, Mrs. Owen, who attended to the woman admitted thht she ot her own know- ledge was aware the woman was given that horrify- ing mixture. Then the" kindly" services of the neighbour disclose a condition of affairs positively monstrous—aye, barbarous ! We talk of Mission- aries to the Black and Yellow ; .why the Missioner has work enough at home, when such case' of abject degradation are to bo unearthed in "Enlightened England in the 19th Century.

The question asked by the jury respecting the provision of a "cisildren's paddling ground" on the Banks of the Manchester Ship Canal reminds me of the magnificent scheme Dr. McDougall some years ago—when the erection of the Victoria-road Board Schools was the topic of the district—sub- mitted. This provided for the excavation of the basement of the school in which suitable swimming baths were to have been erected, and that swim- ming instruction was to have become part of the curriculum. 'Tis an old story, certainly ! But it mnst be refreshing, for the Denominational and Undenominational sections to look back at those times and reflect? In those days, Jr. McDougall's scheme was rejected! Now,we find out the reasons. The Denominational section objected to them "be- cause the schools would be too attractive," and naturally their own would suffer! The undenomina- tional section "objected to the expenses.' Yet, got taunted with the `bogie"Down with the Rates which figured so prominently at the last election. Those were the reasons, and possibly, if they had been as generally reflected upoa as they have since, there would at present be no need for a "paddling ground."

The appointment of Mr. F. A. Lake,solicitor,Run- corn, as clerk to the Justices of the Peace for the Eddisbury Division (Frodshana) comes as a sur- prise to most people though for some time I have suspected that the appointment lay between that gentleman and Mr. A. Browite,solicitor, of Warring- ton, both capable and efficient lawyers. That the honour has tallen to Runcorn is a matter to be proud of and I heartly congratulate Mr. Lake upon his appoin:ment.

Another person to be congratulated is dr. Robert Harold Possnett, The Mount, Mouglands-lane, Run- corn, upon his unopposed return to the representa- tion of Heath Ward on the town governing body. A valuable and sincere friend of Runcorn (tr. P. Speakman) was ruthlessly removed by death, and whilst the town mourns that almost irretrevable loss, it cannot but rejoice that to fitting a person as Mr. Possnett has been secured to fill the vacancy. Mr. Possnett is too well-knoNn to nes,: further re- commendation.

The usual open service of this brotherhood took plare an Sunday last, when the chair WAS taken by Mr. W. Ainsworth, who made a few remarks on " Ornaments in the Church." The speaker, Mr. Millar, gave a capital address on " A Woman's Sacrifice to serve Curist," showing that we ought to do and give the very bast that is possible, even at the cost of envy, jealousy, oppesition, and hatred from others, and irrespective of monet try value, even though it may seem extravagant, if for Christ's sake. Then we may receive approval and the blessing " She hath done what she could." He pointed out many ways how we could break the alabaster box of ointment in public life, by giving old-age pensions, ani minimizing the evils of children's labour, and alleviating the curse of the drink traffic, and in many other instances to do the thing that would be most beneficial to the majority of our fellows around us. THE REV. C. W. L. CHRISTIEN ON CHRISTIAN INDECISION.

In a special sermon delivered to young men, at St. Paul's Wesleyan Cnurca. on Sunday night. the Rev. C. W. L. Christiec (superintendent of the circuit) made an eloquent appeal to his hearers to put away their indeci- sion. Man was forced to choose ! What was it to have a heart like that soeken of by the Psalmist when he said " Unite my he irt to fear Thy name." It was to have what he would call "religious decision." It was not a mere ac- knowledgment; not a regard for the beauty of the services of God that was required. That which on ought to do, aad that which ode chose to do were two different things; and they were compelled to choose the works of God and God's service if they intended to be decided Christians. Tlwy could not be half Christians; they could not hold out ttse hope of peace and comfort to those who were halting and pausing; for the dicision —the choice—the becoiniug a Cnristian—was an irrevocable step, that was to be decided once for all. Yet religion was like health liable to be lost or perman- ently injured if not nurtured and cared for. SONS OP TamnsEun• n RUNCORN.

GOSSIGE'S EMPLOYSX-S' -ANNUAL EXCURSION.

The venue this year, choeen by the customary 'ballot, of the annual excursion of the emplop'o of Messrs. Gus. sage wits the ever popular Lancashire sea-side resort, Blackpool, and to that destination some 1,700 trippers were conveyed by three special trains on Saturday. Promptly to time the trains, loaded with their happy freight, steamed out of the Widnes London and North- Western station. By the time Wigan had been passel, all appearance of disappointing weather had vanished, and the sun shone brightly on the speeding Widnesians. Breezy Siackpool was reached about 10 a.m. The town fully maintained its reputation, and hat guards were found to be very useful. Upon arrival the excursionists lost no time in mingling with the members of the other 41 excursions to the town, and seeking pleasure out of the thousind and one avenues open to them at every turn. The tide being in, the majority wandered down the promenade to watch the play of the water, or else rambled on to the piers to listen to the various orchestral performances. The sea b Aug rough, no boats were out, and consequently the excur- sionists were debarrei the pleasure of yachting or row- ing, but all the other places of amusement were well patronized, and the shops, displaying their fancy wares and mementoes of the gay town of pleasure, were not forgotten. The Tower was a centre of attraction, and many ascended to the dizzy height of the upper plat- forms despite the boisterous wind. The Great Wheel, Winter Gardens, and the Alhambra, all came in for their share of attention, as well as the numerous shows upon the beach. As six o'clock approechel the excursioniets turned t'neir feet towerds the stetion, well satisfied with their day's outing, and laden w.th the spoils of the sea and the ioevitable Blackpool rock. Undoubtedly every- body enjoyed themselves to the.full,and the indefatigable and praiseworthy efforts of Mr. Puysh contributed in no small degree to this end. The journey home was made quickly and safely, the lase train arriving in Widnes at 9 p.m. Mr. McConnell, the Widnes station mast r, is also dezerving of commendation for the intoner in which the rsilway arrangements were, carried out. Acording to custom, the empleees received their ususl berms, viz. men is 6d and boys is each, t 3 defray their expenses.

A WOMAN S TERRIBLE END AT RUNCORN. A HUSBAND AND MIDWIFE AND NEIGHBOUR CONCERNED.

At the Runcorn Police Court, on Monday, Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner, held an inquest upon the body of Harriet Wakefield, wife of John Wakefield, waterman, who died on Saturday night, under extramdinary cir- cumstances.consequent upon a recent confinement.—Mr. Ellis Gatley was foreman of the jury.—The police were represented by Superintendent Pearson and Inspector Hicks.

John Wakefield, waterman, said he resided at 33, Fryer-street, R incorn. Deceased was his wife, and 37 years of age. His wife was confined on Tuesday the 20:11 inst., whilst he was away with his boat. The mid- wife attending her was Jane Owen. He was at home on the Thursday and Friday and saw the mid-wife. As far as he could see his wife seemed to be going on all right, anyhow he was not told anything by Mrs. 0 .ven net would indicate his wife was not doing well. On Saturday morning his wife died. He sent for the mid-wife and also for a doctor. Dr. Robin- sni was first celled in, but he believed that Dr. McDougall came afterwards. As far as he knew his wife had no drink during her confinement. The reason he did not go to see his wile was because his work kept him away. He went away on the Saturday morning and returned about 4 o'clock OR Saturday afternoon. A man told him that his wife was dead somewhere be- tween time and four o'clock. He did not go home to see her on hearing the news,but stopped at his captain's house because he was so upset.—The Coroner : I should think if you were upset; you would have gone home to see abut it ?—Witness : I blame myself for that. I ought to have dene. lint I was so upsese—The Coroner : Then it was a nice way of showing your upsettedness!— A Juror : Have you been living apart from your wife P—No, sir.—How long have you been living to- gether ; and how long in Fryer-street P--We have been living together about 14 years, and in Fryer-street about 2i Years. Jane Owen. an elderly woman, of Runcorn, said she had been out many years as a mid-wife, but was not certified. She attended the deceased during confinement and had been to the house several times a day up to the time of death. Deceased appeared to be going oa all rieht, only she had a bad cough. Witness wanted her to have a doctor in on the Wednesday to toe baby ; and also wanted her to send for a doctor for herself and the baby on the Thursday. Witness sent for Dr. Robinson to comets the baby, but no doctor came. Deceased told witness she del not require a doctor, as she had been that way before—it was an old couele. On Saturday morning the husband came to her house, and said the deceased was worse. She went immediately and also sent for Dr. Robinson, but got no answer. She sent four different people to request Dr. Robinson to come. Subeequeatly she went to fetch Dr. McDougall. He came at something after nine o'clock and did all lie could. He ordered de- ceased some nourishment but she died about half-past eleven o'clock. Witness sent for the doctors, but would not be answerable to them for fees and there was a diffi- culty in getting doctors when people could not pey the fees.—Pressed by the Coroner witness stated she sent on Wednesday for the doctor to came to the baby ; but it was Saturday morning when she sent for a doctor for the woman : that would be after the husband had been to see her. The deceased seemed to ge worse on Friday.—Then why did you not send for a doctor on Friday P—Witness : She would not have one.—Did you speak to her about it? I spoke to her on Wednesday and begged her to send for a doctor -to give her something for her colt— Why did she not have a doctor? She said she would , not have one, as she had been like that all the winter. —Continuing, witness said that deceased said she had ' had coughs and bad bouts for some time. She had also had diarrhera very badly all the time before she was called in. Witness wanted her to have something to ease it. She drank some buttermilk and beer. Witness I knew deceased had had some beer .and accused her of it.—By Superintendent Pearson :—Did you ever see her the worse for liquor P—Witness : Since Tuesday? Oh! no. She had, however, seen deceased have some bitter beer.—Had you reason to think she had too much ? I could not say because her speech was going a little. I could not understand her very well. It was on Thurs- day or Friday.—The Coroner : Did you not think, see- ing her speech was going on Thursday you should have insisted upon her having a doctor ?—Yes, sir. I told her on several occasion.. She had had drink, but witness could not say who had fetched it for her.—Tbe Coroner: Don't you know that when you take upon yourself the attention of a woman in her confinement, von assume a very responsible position P—Witness : Yes, sir, and I always do my duty.—The Coroner: Had you done so on Thursday you would have sent for a doct sr.—Witness : Well, sir, they will not come without tieing paid.—Dr. McDougall observed that that was hardly true. He did not think there was a doctor in the district who would have refused te see the woman, had they been requested, and the statement of the wit. , nese was a libel upon doctors generally. By Superintendent Pearson : During the time you were attending the woman did vou see anyone else there P—Witness : I saw Mrs. Restock there.—With regard to Mrs. Restock's condition on Saturday was she drunk or sober P—l did not see her.—After several evasive replies witness stated that Mrs. Bost )ck was of intemperate habits. Witness had not seen Mrs. Bostock fetch drink, but she had seen a little boy fetch morns rum and beer. The lad did not fetch whiskey as deceased did not take it. By the Coroner : What state was Mrs. Bostock in ?-- Witness : She was sober inough.—The Coroner : If she was sober enough how can you account for the doctor ordering her out of the house.—Witness : That was in the afternoon and not whilst I was there.—The Coroner: You knew that beer and rum were being taken into the house. Did you try and see the husbend and tell him what was going on P—Witness : On the Friday after- noon i went up and waited to see Dr. McDougall to tell him the COUSB4uences of it,but I did not see the husband. —Ernest Wakefield, son of Charles Wakefield, and nephew of the deceased, said he resided with his uncle John Wakefield ever since be was two years old. He was now 13 years of age. He knew the circumstances of his aunt'a death. His aunt had drink. Mrs. Bostock frequently came to the house, and he had fetched ' whiskey, beer, and rum, on several occasions. Jane Owen WSB in the house also, and knew lee fetched the liquor. His uncle and aunt lived together always, that he knew of. But there were four or five other men also residing in the same house. Questioned by Dr. Mc- D eugall as to whether he had been to school, the lad re- plied in the affirmative. He used to go to Trinity and the Board Schools, and almost all of them. He had not been looked up by Mr. Bowyer, and had probably slipped the school authorities' _memory. He bad passed the second standard.—The lad signed his depositien in remarkably good hand-writing for a lad of standard two a subject both the Coroner and Dr. McDougall commen- ted upon. Constable George Carrington stated that he had known the parties for about three years. The husband had been living apart from his wife and they only come together about a twelve-month previously. The deceased woman was of very intemperate Dr. Findlay McDougall said as far as he could remem- ber he had never attended the deceased before, but that wag a point upon which he could not be quite sure. Dr. Robinson came to him a little after nine o'clock on the Saturday and made a statement to him about the present case and requested witnecs to accompany him. Dr. Robinson said it was a case of gross neglect, an opinion he (Dr. McDougall), confirmed entirely. Dr—Robinson said the woman was dying. They went in company to the house and up- stairs he found the woman in the condition Dr. Robinson had described. The house appeared to be destitute of anything that a person in deceased's condition ought to have had. He, however, arranged that what was re- quisites should be supplied forthwith. They met the Parish Nurse at the gate of his braise and she accom- panied them down to the house. He saw the mid-wife then, and she appeared sober enough. He also saw a deaf man and S. couple of others on the floor breaking sticks. They said that the husband left that morning and the impression left upon him (witness) was that the husband acted very meanly. They (the doctors) were anxious to do all they could, and he would again say they as doctors were compelled to make great sacrifices. —The Coroner observed he did not think there was any class in the community who made such sacrifices for the sake of the community as doctors did. And he had, in his experience, had to deal with a great number of cases.—Dr. McDaugall, centinuing,said he knew that Dr. Robinson was much dietreased, as it was a case that he thought would have to be investiga- ted to see whether the responsibility for the state of affairs could be laid upon anyone. He addressed some few words to the deceased, but the woman would not speak to him at all, and there was considerable diffi- colts, in getting to know her true condition. Still the woman was not absolutely unconscious. He would go Flo far as to say that he considered that the mid-wife had acted no to her rights, end anything she may have dole was owing entirely to ignorance of what she ought to no. The cause of death would be blood poisoning, in some form. In his opinion if a doctor had been called in on the Thursday there might have been a reasonable chance of the wonsan's recovery. With regard to the "whisky and rum" he considered it was a very repre- hensible proceeding. He saw the woman Bosteck in the house and knowing her to be no fit woman te be near the deceased he had made " no bones "about bundling her out. At that time, however, he did not know the deeessed was deed, as it was on a subst'quent visit, he turned the woman out.

The Coroner asked Mrs. Bostock whether she wished to give evidence, and upon 11:.r electing to do so she es i 4 she was the wife of James Bostock and resided at 16, Alcock-street, Ruccorn. She visited the deceased 'very day until her death.—The Coroner : Did yrau send the boy Ernest on the afternoon of the confinement tor some rum P—Witness : I have seen her drink brtter beer.—The Coroner : That is not the ques- tion. It is said :hat you have been drunk in deceased's bedroom P—Witnetis : That is not true : No,

sir.—The Coroner : Is it true that: you have had drink there P—Witness : It is that little boy's neglect ;he did not look after tiQr. I am the mother of a large family myself.--ifter further evasive answers, the wit- ness continuing said : The deceased spoke to her the same as usual on Friday. Mrs. Owen was trying to persuade her to have a doctor. The husband was there on the Tuesday and Wednesday night 3, but she could not remember whetiaer he was there on the Thursday night. She considered the husband had done his duty as any other man would have done it. She could not tell what had become of the half-pint of brandy left by the doctor, but thought the old man had it.—By Super- intendent Pearson : She was well aware she was upon her oath. She had not been drinking w;th the decmsed daily. She did come to the Police Office and make a deposition to Police-Constable Carrington. She did not say to the officer that she was" drinking daily" but that" a pint of beer cams to the house daily." She did not attend to the deceased ; there was another per- son doing that.—The Coroner : Have you never 10-2 en drunk in your house P—Witness : My husband was at home.--k Juror: Is it not a fact thit you gave the de- ceased 891116 spirits on Thursday afternoon P—Witness : I bad no money for that. We were hard up ourselves at the end of the week—The Superintendent : Did the decbased give you any money P—Witness: No.

Dr. McDougall: They tell me that the brandy was divided between the people, and there is uo doubt she had a share too.

The Coroner: Just so. In summing up Mr. Worsiey said to his mind it revealed a very shocking state of affairs. It showed the heartlessness of the husband and he coed not conceive of any man acting in that way toward the woman he had made his wife. As to the midwife, Dr. McDougall had said good naturedly that she had acted to her rights. Well he would accept that. There had been some talk of a bill being brought into Parliament treating with the registration of naid-wives, and he thought that case was one that brought very strong evidence upon that subject. There was no doubt that upon a mid- wife taking possession of a case in that way, where there was no doctor in attendance, it was her strict duty to warn not only the patient, but the husband, and who ever may be in the house of the gravity of the case, and upon the case going beyond the grasp of the mid-wife, a doctor should be called. There could be little doubt that the woman was of intemperate habits, and there could be little doubt but that the woman, Rostock, who had decided to give her evidenc was drunk, which was a most disgraceful state of things. Nothing was provided for the woman, either due to her I own intemperate habits, or to the neglect of others. It was a shocking sate of things, especially in the face of the fact that the doctor was of opinion that had medical aid been called in from the Wednesday there would have been a chance of her recovery. He felt so strongly that he would ask the jury to censure every- one concerned with the exception of the doctor and police. The husband had neglected his wife grossly, I and the mid-wife must have known of the drinking that was going on and also the neighbour who went in to drink with her. Something had been said with regard to the doctors being unwilling to come without their fees. In his experience, however,it was one of the very , last things a doctor looked to. He did not know any class of men who were more seif-sacrificing. and willing to do anything for the poor people, attending them even when there was act the slightest hope of any payment. He did not think they would attach any importance to that aspersien upon the doctors. Indeed, the statement contradicted itself, and in fixing upon Dr. Robinson and Dr. McDougall—both of whom were well known for the amount of good they did for the poor—the illustration was an obvious contradiction of Itself. He advocated the bringing in of a verdict of death from natural causes —probably blood poisoning, and if the jury liked to add anything in the way of censure they could do so. After a lengthy retirement the jury brought in the following verdict :—" The deceased has probably died from natural causes—blood poisoning. The jury are of the opinion that the deceased has contractei her death by her own intemperate habits, and great blame is due to the midwife and husband for not calling in a doctor, and that an Act should be passed for the registration of mid-wives. Mrs. Rasteck, we also desire, should be censured for her conduct In the case."—The Coroner: I heartily agree with you. The Coroner (to the husband): lam sorry to say that the verdict of the jury is one which is very disgraeef al for you to hear. It is a very !disgraceful 'thing for me to have to say to you that you being the husband of this woman, on hearing of her death never went to inquire about her. The jury consider that your conduct has been wholly reprehensible and request me to censure you and I do so for your neglect to your wife.—To the told-wife: You have been a mid-wife in Runcorn for many years, and I hope I may never see you here upon a similar case again. If I do, most assuredly, you will be most severely dealt with. The jury considerthat you should have informed everyone about the house that a ' doctor was necessary, and not only that, you should I have seen that a doctor was sent for. When you take the charge of a woman in her confinement you are putting yourself in a responsible position. You are occupying a position which should be occupied by a medical man, and if anything goes wrong you are liable to be called in question. In future when you think a doctor is re- quired you must certainly at once see that a doctor is got; and if the persons are not in a position to pay for medical attendance, you must see that the medical officer of the district is communicated with in a proper way. Your conduct Is very censurable.—To Mrs. toak : I did not want to call you and therefore asked you whether you would like to give evidence. You de- sired it, otherwise. I should not have called you, for I consider in the evidenee you have given, you have not given it truthfully. You gave your evidence on oath. I am sorry to say I cannot accept it as being true. I be- lieve that y, ea and this women (deceased) were drink- ing in the house together. I believe you did that know- ing that the woman was ill and that you were in a thorough state of recklessness and did not do what you should have done as a neighboer. You are very greatly censurable. Censurable for drinking with a woman re- cently confined, and that too in face of the fact that you yourself have had children ; you are censurable for not having told the husband ; and von are censurable also as the person who should have known better. I hope I shall never see you here again. The Coroner added he would not allow those witnesses any expenses.

THE LIGHTING OF RUNCORN LOCKS;

LETTER FROM MR. JON MEADOWCROFT.

At the inquest held at the Runcorn Police Court, on Monday afternoon, the Coroner (Mr. J. E. Woreley), in- formea the jury that, as requested at the recent inquiry into the death by drowning of Charles Barton, a War- rington law student, he had communicated with Mr. Meadowcroft, as representing the Ship Canal Company, and he received the following reply :—.‘ I beg to ac- knowledge the receipt of your letter of the 15th inst. and the same shall have my best attention. Perhaps I may say that the watchman has the discretion of lif!hting the lamps if he think 4 the darkness calla for i. On Oa night of the unfortunate occurrence I was on the lock side myself at 11.5 p.m., and within 300 yards of the mishap, and it was not what I should call dark. At all events, with ordinary care, a parson could have crowd the locks safely, espeeially over the footboarl which leads to Bridgewater House. It is a good broad ere,and fitted up to make the creasing as sife RS plisible. The gate, which it was thought the deceased was attempting to cross RR a footbaard, is simply to allow for boatmen working the locks, and the bottom gates are not so favourable f3r inexperierced per4oo4 to gross as this one. Had he gone over the proper foot way he would have incurred no risk whatever. Never- theless. I feel extremely sorry that such a misfortune should have arisen, and I am willing to do what is possible and practical to guard against accident, and I may say I have instructed the watchman not to hesitate. to light the lamps if he considers the weather justifies it. The months of June and July are the months dur- ing which we clean the lamps" The Coroner said he was sure the jury would be glad the recommendation they made had received attention. The Jury thanked the Coroner and several inquired whether anything further had been heard with respect to the fencing off of a portion of the Ship Canal by Ferry Hut for the purpose of a bathing place for the young folk of the town. The Coroner observed that he had also received a letter respecting that, but the matter was not quite ripe yet.

RUNCORN B9ATING CLUB.

Mr. Rigby—the well-known photographer—of Runa corn, was the guest of the Runcorn Boating club, on Saturday afternoon, when a fair number ef members turned up at ths B ,at HOUSO, and aftr‘r the boats were photographed on the water, proceeded up the river, and there choo.ing on of the many pretty wooded spots on the banks of the Weaver, a zroup was transferred to dry plate. The rowing from this point to Dutton Lock, three miles further on, was cmipatitive, each boat try- ing to maintain the lead. The " Nora," however arrived at the Lock first, carrying for her crew—quite a coincidence—the Secretary ; Treisurer ; Captain; and sub-Captain ; which fact rends to prove that the officials of the club cultivate physical excellence, as well as the mental powers exercised in the manage- ment of the club affairs ; which judging by results are exercised to advantage. After tea the members indulged in running, and :jumping fr s•nall prizes coctributed amongst themselves. The most notable feature of which was the high backward jump by Mr. W Thomas, he clearing a fairly high hedge row, winning the first prize amid deserved applause. Th!spoat house was reached about 8.45, where,af ter a delimits plunge in the tepid waters of the Weaver, the members expressed •their atisfaction with the outing.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES.

AN INTERESTING POINT WITH REGARD TO EGGS.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Friday. before the Mayor (in the chair) and other magistrates, Patrick Sexton, junior, was summoned at the instance of Inspector Norman for emptying rstten eggs on the public street on June 14.—Mr. Powell said about 10 minutes past five on the day in question he bad occasion to cross Sharp•srreet and saw a cart opposite a shop and CICB3 by were two or three dozen rotten eggs on the ground. When defendant came out of the shop witness asked him why he had thrown the eggs there. Defend- ant replied he did not think he was doing any harm and picked the shells up with the help of a boy, but the con- tents were left on the ground.—By Mr. Browne (representing defendant) : The eggs were rotten ; be could smell them.—Mr. Browne said the eggs were not rotten, It was one of the most paltry CAS33 he had ever had to appear in. The defendant was taking eggs out of a case in his cart and putting them into a basket, and in doing so found several broken ones ; and in order to get out other ones picked out the broken ones ando put them on the street temporarily because he had n place to put them on his cart. It was impossible to find a rotten egg at thia time of the year amongst Irish ones.—Mr. Patrick Sexton, senior, egg merchant, said it was a fact that there were no rotten eggs at this period of the year.—By Mr. Eoberts : Be could tell a rotten egg by the feel of it.—Mr Roberts said he knew it was passible to tell by shaking an egg if it were froth but did not know one could *tell by th 3 feel.—Mr. Sexton adhered to his statement.—The Chairman said they could not allow the case to pass and defendant was fined' in the costs-6s. 6d.

DEFRAUDING TEN RUNCORN PUBLIC AND ASSAULTING

THH POLICH.-FOUR MONTHS' IMPHISONMHNT.

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday. before Messrs. John Ockleston (presidine)and Francis Wiswall, a case of obtaining money under false pretences of rather an ingenious nature was heard.—John Inglefield, of Lostock Oralarn,was charged with unlawfully collect beg alms under certain false pretenses, and also with assaulting Police Constable Bratt whilst in the execution ot his duty,on the previous Saturday.—Prieoner pleaded guilty to both charges.—The evidence of Constable Alfred Brett, Frederick Pearson. Greenway-road ; James Stevens, Egerton-street and Charles Whoeley, parcels agent of L. and N. W. Railway Company, disclosed the following facts. The man Inglesfield went about with a petition worded to the following effect: "Kind friends please read over the leaf." And upon turning over it continued: "Mr. John Inglesfield has a great bereave-; ment in his house at the present time hio wife and child having died. He is very anxious ty bury them decently, and will be very grateful for ana evidence you can kindly give him." Then followed zeenlie- ef si,eneSuree evidently in the same handwrit- ing as ti,e Petal° ; and then a nunlber of apparently genuine ones "Mrs. Coward" is; and numeroas "Friends "for varying sums from 31 to Is. The con- stable's attention was called to the man, and as he emerge' out of a shop in Regent-street, Brett asked him to see the paper he was begging with. The man sub- mitted the papers (produced), an i said he lived in Sur- rey-street. Brett said he would go to Surreysstreet, and ascertain whether the facts were correct, and they started off in company. As they proceeded the man said " He lived in Holloway-road "so they proceeded in that direction. When passing under the railway bridge prisoner made as if to run away. but Brett brought him to with a " Not 30 fast my man." Prisoner then said : " Look here it is all a farce. aqd I will give you 21 to let me go." Bran replied he could not take bribes, and would have t arrest him. Inglesfield then said "There is not a policeman in Ruuc3rn that would take me." Brett quietly reciliel " Well, I will have to try." Prisoner then struck at the Censt able, and then there was a scuffle in which both tell to the ground. Prisoner bit the officer in tbe wrict, and other- wise assaulted him. In r 6,sponse to the officer's whistle, , however assistance came 'n the steeps of a couple rat the Railwae Co.'s empleyees. Subsequently prisoner was handcuffed, but owing to his persistent refusal to move he had eventually to be taken to the police station in a conveyance, the London and North-Westsrn Railway Co.'s parcel van being reqeieitioned to play the rote of "Black Maria."—Prisoner's wife appeared in court, and said the was his second wife. His children were alive and one of them was in court. They only came into Rnncern from Lostock on the previous Wednesday and had been staying at Cobbler's Lodging House, Fryer-street.—The Cnairman observed that it was a great shame of the prisoner to go abeut in that fraudulent way ; as such tricks caused people to he suspicious of even deserving people. They would take notice of that, and for obtaining the money under fa:se pretences he wonici have to go to prison for 3 mon'hs, For the assault on the police, he would be imprisoned for one month. Tbe sentences to run consrcavively. The money 7s 3d found on his person (the evident proceeds of his fraud), would go towards his maintenance in prison.

ERECTION OF WILSON-PATTEN MEMORIAL TABLETS AT THE PARISH

CHURCH.

During the past week two memorial tablets have been erected in the Patten Chapel of the Parish Church, Warrington. The tablets are of polished brass miunteq on ebony, and are tbb work of M(ssrs.Shrigley and Runt, of Lancaster and Lon- don. The inscription on the tablet immediately opposite the marble effigy of the late Lord Wininar- leigh reads as follows :—" The marble effigy of the Right Honourable John Baron Winmarleigh resting on the monument erected by him to the memory of his wife was added in loving remembrance of their paren's by their surviving daughter:! Ellinor and Elizabeth Wilson-Patten, ad, 1891." The second tablet, which is much larg,r, bears the names of those interred in the vault below and which has now been finally closed. The inscription reads "In the vault beneath this chapel lie buried the bodies of Thomas Patten died 21st February, 1772. aged 82: Richard Patten died 6th December, 1774, aged 2 years and 4 months; Dorothea, wife of Thomas Patten died 26th July,l799,aged 65; Thomas Patten, died 19th March, 1806. aged 75 ; Peter Patten, horn Ist June, 1808, died let June, 1808; Thnrnas Wilson-Patten, died 5h December, 1827. aged 57; Ann Maria Patten, died 10 Decm')er, 1837, aged 80 year; ; Anna Maria, wife of John Wilson-Patten, died 4 August. 1816, aged 45; Elizabeth Wilson-Patten. died 5 June 1871, aged 93: Eustane John Wilson-Patten, died 17 December, 1873, azed 37 : Jol,n Wilson-Patten (created Baron Winraarleigh, 1874), died 11 July 1892, aged 90 The vault was filled up and finally closed 14th July, 1892.

LATCHFORD TEMPERANCE HALL.

On Saturday afternooon, the Good Templets of the " (leaner" Lodge's Cycling Club, Stockport, paid a visit to Latchford. Tea was provided in the Temperance Hall by the Latchford friends, after which two open-air meetings, which were well attended, were held, and a number of pledges were taken. On re-assembling in the Temperance Hall a vote of thanks was proposed by Bro. J. Corbett, chief templet., seconded and supported by Coun- cillor Ellison, co the Stockport Club. The latter commenced their retnrn journey at 830 p.m. On ‘londay evening the usgal weekly meeting was held in the Temperance Hall, presided over by Bro. J. Corbett.

PRESENTATION AT THE LEAGUE HALL.

_ A very interesting ceremony took nlace on Sun- day evening in the League Hall, Bewsev—treet, Warrington. in the form of a presentation, from the boys ot the junior bawl, to their band-master Mr. M. Flannery; The present consisted of a silver. mounted pipe and case along with pouch, tobacco, and match box. Rev. F. Mercer, D. 0., 0.5.8., chaplain, president of the League made the presentation en behalf of the boys to Mr. Flannery and referred to the energetic and splendid manner in which Mr. Flennery had worked the boys up during the last six months. It was very gratifying to him(Father Mercer) to see that the boys appreciated the good that had been done for them, both in keeping them from roving about the streets at night and also teaching them music. Mr. Flannery was giving all his spare time for their benefit and they ought to be proud of him, and do all that they could to keep him with them. In giving Mr. Flannery the present the Chaplain saiol that he hoped Mr. Flannery would use the pipe un- til he (not the pipe) got to be a good old age but be was afraid be would have to buy more to- bacco. Mr. Flannery in acknowledging the presents said that be never expected such a present from the boys that he had under his control, but it showed to him that what little he had done for them they had not forgeren it,and if his health was snared he would try and make them the finest Catholic junior drum and fife band in this part of the country. But he must not forget the very able and strong Committee that was working for them, and to these noble and hard working men he muet return his most hearty thanks, for making the boys' band what it was. He must also not for- get to thank Mr. J. J. Whittaker for his very valu- able advice and assistance in forming the junior band; and thanks must he given to the manager and manageress (Mr. and Mrs. McCormack) for their help to these boys.

SALE OF WORK AND GARDEN FETE AT THE RECTORY.

By the kind permission of thP Rector, a sale of work and garden fate were commenced in the Rectory grounds on Thurs lay last week in connec- tion with the First) C:inrch, in aid ot Home and Foreign Missions. Two large teats had been pitched in the grounds immediately behind the Rectory, one being used for the holding of a sale of useful and ornamental articles, flowers, etc., and the other as a refreshment bluret. It was in the former that the opening Ceremony was performed before a fairly-large company shortly after three o'clock on Thu .sday afternoon. The fete champetre comprised amusements and enter- taiamenta ot an enjoseable cheracter. There were tennis and croquct tournaments, as well as a bicycle gym- khana, maypole dance, tug-ot-war, quoits, putting the weight, and dancing. The Blue Coat &hoot brass band was in aleedaece and played numerous selections. The arrangemosits tor the fete were in the capable hands of the Key. J. W. Dickenson and the Rev. A. S. Cameron. Ainceigs.,. those present at the opening ceremony were the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis), the Yen. Archdeacon MacCarthy (late Arehdeacon ot Calcutta), Revs Canon Penrhyn, Morley Stevenson, B Percival, E P Weatherhead, G W Dickenson, & F White,A S Cameron, El A Lister, and W Bracecamp. The RECTOR, in the course of a few introductory re- marks said most of those present would be aware that in. 1895 and 1898 they had two large bazaars at each of which they raised over £l3OO for schools, church and missions. Owing to them they thought it well to drop their usual summer sales of work which where begun directly after he came there eleven years ago. For the first three years their sales were for home and foreign missions and they realised from £6O to £7O per annum. rhey found it necessary however, owing to their in- creasing parochial expenditure to devote the proceeds of their summer sale to parochial purposes and for the next three years the proceeds were given to various objects in the parish re- alising Lloo, ano, and £2OO. They were now re- suming the summer sales again and he was glad to say that they were in such a pesition as to be again unselfish and were going to devote the proceeds te home and foreign missions. There were about ten societies they wished to help including the Christian Knowledge Society the National Society, Additional Curate Society, Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts, the Bazaars of Calcutta, Universities Mission to Central Africa, Mersey Mission to Seamen and the Warrington Day Nursing Institute• ' Tae last named was the only one which could be considered selfish but was entirely outside their own parochial work. When he looked back on the past and saw what had been done at similar sales, he hoped that the profits of their parochial sale this year might raise; at least £lOO. He felt sure that he would not appeal in vain. (Applause.) Archdeacon MACCARTHY in speaking of mission work in India, said anyone who had spent, as he had spent, 25 years in India, could not fall to be deeply interested in the country. India had claims upon them ail English people, and English Church people. It did a great deal for us. In the first place it was the great training place for our army. They must have been struck with the extraordinary and phenomenal success of the British Army in different warlike enterprises where other nations seemed to fail. A great deal of the Success which had attended our military operations in different parts of the globe was due to the fact that the British army got such splendid training in India. They also owed much to the splendid native army of Inelia,which owned allegiance to the Queen,and loyally obeyed her behests. India also provided a career for ',thousands of England's* eons in the army, civil service, medical service, and various mercantile and banking operations. There were thousands of English families with sons in India who found a career there which would perhaps have been denied to them in this country where competition is keen and opportunities comparatively few. In a great business centre like Warrington he ought also to say that India conferred upon them the great advantage of an immense market for our goods. He had seen the goods of Manchester, Birmingham and Sheffield on the little stalls of the native dealers and the familiar Eng- lish names on the packets containing them. Let bias now mention some of the things given to India. We had secured for the natives of India justice between man and man. Justice was dispensed by the English civil servants very differently from the way it was be- fore Britede rule. We had given pretectiou to men of every creed and every class ; and we had also given a splendid system of education. By the establish- ment of elementary schools and universities in great centres of population the Indians pad become acquainted with a great deal of Western science and Western knowledge. The question was were we to stop short of that. Surely it was our duty to give them the highest gift of all, that which had made Bug and Prosperous and happy, a knowledge of the Saviour, Jesus Christ. It was impossible for the Government te meddle with the religions of the country or directly to promote missionary work. It was the duty and privi- lege of the Christian Church to do that. People some- times said they had heard of missionary enterprise for many years but did not hear much in the way of success. They pointed to the huge population and the comparatively small number of Christians, and seemed to think that no headway was being made. The speaker proceeded to refer to the great extensions in missionary enterprise which he had witnessed in India, and said he firmly believed that the preparatory work new being done was undermining the sceptism of India, and the next century would see these millions brought into the fold of Christ. (Appiaute.)

The ARCHDEACON'S services were acknoswied2ed on the motion of the Rev. Canon Peorhyn, seconded by Mr. Dewhurst. _

The takings during the day realised £4O.

On Saturday the Sale of Work and Garden Fête in aid of the Rome and Foreign Missions, was continued from Thursday, the opening ceremony being performed by the Rev. A. N. Banorjei, Cathedral Missionary, Calcutta. There was an excellent attendance.

The RECTOR said they had bad -a very successful day on Thursday, and he hoped together with the proceeds of that day they would raise £lOO. It was his desire to give El 0 to each of ten societies. The Rev. A. N. Beemieter, in declaring the sale of work open, said it was a great privilege to come once more to Warrington on an occasion of that kind. He knew they were all interested in the cause of Home and Foreign Missions, They had had enough of Missionary addresses in the past, but he would like to say a little about India, a subject upon which he could speak with authority, for he had spent 25 years there. There was a treat important work to be done in India, and the time had come when a great effort must be made to promulgate Christian principles throughout the land. An irnportant part of the werk was to bring closer together all classes of tbe people, Social life in India and England was very different. In fact for the most part it was at its worst in India' and the great work of the Christian Church should be to effect an improvement. He was pleased to be able to say that the people of India were becoming better educated, and what be regarded as a notable sign, took a very deep interest in sports. That was all a movement in the right direction, and having a beneficial influence on the social life of the community, did a great amount of good. The first work of the Church must be among the native Chris- tians : There were only three on of Christians out lof three hundred millions of inhabitants. The Church bad the duty of converting two hundred and ninety- seven millions of people in India. The important part of the work would be done by the people. He was asso- ciated with the work of the Christian community, and he could not over-estimate the importance of the duties that lay before them. It was his privilege to welcome the Rector on his recent visit to Calcutta, where he saw their boys and girls beine taught much in the same way as the boys and girls in England were taught. It was their great hope that they wonld in after years take a. lead in various departments of life and do good by influ- ence and ex imple. He asked those present to support the foreign wissions to the best of their ability. The Rev, F. E Poweee also spoke in support of the objects of the sale. On the motion of Mr. DENVIIIIRST, secende I by the Rev. A. F. WHITE, a vote of thanks was passed to the Rev. A. N. Banerjei, and tho business of the day was then proceeded with. The programme of amusements and entertainments provided for the afternoon was most interesting and as the weather was so fine the proceedings were throughly eej )yed. In the eveniog the Volunteer band played for dancing.

THE PROCEEDS. Saturday's proceeds amounted to £4l 144 id, making the total proceeds of th 3 two days' sales £7B us. 6a. This money will be given to the Home and Foreign Miqsions.

MA.RRIAGE OF MR. W. H. PA.YION,

At St. Peter's Church, West Bromwich, last Thursday, the marriage was solemnized of Mr. W H. Payton, Mus. Bac.,of Warrington, and Miss Mary Beckett, of Albion, West Bromwich. A very large congregation assembled to witness the ceremony which was performed by the Vicar, the Bev. Henry Jesson. Shortly before two o'clock the wedding party arrived at the church and during the interval of waiting for the bride, the organist, Mr. J. Brown, F.G.0., played a selection of music. Precisely at two o'clock the bride arrived accompanied by Mr. It. J. Richardson (cousin) whp gave her away. She was attired in a lovely whit& Indian silk dress (a present from her brother, Dan, in India) with veil and orange blossoms. There were six bridesmaids in attendance, wearing pale green dresses covered , with white muslin and trimmed with white lace and chiffon with hats to match. The bride carried a beautiful bouquet of flowers as did each of the bridesmaids, the bouquets being presents of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were Miss Fanny and Miss Annie Beckett(sisters of the bride), Miss Polly Payton (sister of the bridegroom), Miss Richardson (cousin of the bride), Miss Ethel Payton and Miss Annie Bray (neices of the bride). Mr. Harry Beckett, brother of the bride, acted as best man. The ceremony passed oft with ecat and after the signing of the register the happy poir left the church to the strains of the "Wedding March" performed with much spirit by the organist. The reception and wedding breakfast were held at the residence of the bride. Amongst the guests present were: Mrs Beckett (mother of the bride), Miss Payton (aunt of the bridegroom), Mr and Mrs W Beckett, Mr and Mrs J Beckett, Mr and Mrs Harris,Mr.Mrs and Miss Richardson (Birmingham), Mr and Mrs Bray (Cleobury Mortimer),Mrs and Miss Lewis, Mrs and Miss Roberts (Wednesbury), Mr H Griffiths, Mr W Roberts and others. The health of the bride and bridegroom was proposed in happy terms by Mr Richardson, and the bridegroom suit- ably responded. The newly-married couple left early in the evening for Malvern, where the honey- moon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was a handsome fawn costume with fawn hat trimmed with pink. SOMETHING ABOUI

"THE REVENGE OF VALERIE."

" THE REVENGE OF VALERIE" is an exciting novel about Klendyke which has solid foundations of fact. Among MR. NISBET'S intimate friends is an expert gold digger from Australia who was (mat of the pioneers of vld-mining in British Columbia.' and has kept in touch with the most stirring events and even the smaller details of existence out there, which both of them from their earlier Austral experi- ences are able to appreciate as no ordinary observer could. The love theme of the story is strong and the characters are drawn with skill and strength,induced by a study of human nature in many lands. Sir Lewis Eastlake is introduced to the reader, when, at twenty-four years of age, he is going the pace in a London Gambling Club. An altercation there causes a duel at Cherbourg in which his opponent is killed, Sir Lewis has been accompanied by his Club com- panion, Captain Eric St. Arnoul,who has him nursed at the house of a Cherbourg young dector,an acquaint- ance of some years, with a pretty sister who does the nursing. The story, however, centres on the passion of a woman for a man rather than on that of a man for a woman. The beautiful and wealthy Duchess Valerie has from motives of affectiou been finding Sir Lewis Eastlake with money through a financier, and when she learns of his illness, she arranges with her cousin, Captain St. Arnoul, for the invalid's removal to her seat at Calais,the Captain to be his escort and companion. She thinks that now it will all end well. little imagining that Sir Lewis has before the duel been privately married to the doctor's sister, his engagement to her having been of some standing. Sir Lewis on his recovery goes alone to seek a fortune for himself and wife at Klondyke, and while he is encountering adventures there, the Duchess, on a reaction of sentiment for him, cruelly plots to ruin the character of his wife, and so bring about a divorce to be followed by her own marriege to hin. The Duchess attains her triumph, but the girl has a counter revenge which makes a striking climax.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors cf let!-3rs intended for publication must in al case-3 send their names and addre;ses to the &Jinn Meters intendcd for publication must b 3 writtEn on one side only of the pqer. Correspondents are strongly advisnl to write briefly. A short letter has much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

SUNDAY TRiDING. Sir,—The latest local pronouncemeut on this most important subject that I have read is to b 3 f aund in a sermon preached by the Vicar of St. Paul's and repro- duced in your journal of last week. There are some points in the sermon about which something may be said. At the outset, I must differ with the preacher on a point of fact. Mr. Tarbuck says that" those who were most inclined to cause work to be dole on Sunday were not the workers but those who " neither toil nor spin." There are, unfortunately, only too many people in the idle and wealthy classes against whom this charge may be levelled. But they certainly are not the only sinners in this respect. A scrutiny of the passengers who crowd the Sunday trains and omni- buses, reveals the painful fact that the great majority of the Sunday trippers belong to the working classes. And many thoteands of railway servants and carriage drivers are thus deprived of the day of rest by the in- censiderate conduct or fellow workers. Mr. Tarbuck tells us that "he had made it his business to see several publicans during the last few weeks, and be had found that there was a feeling amongst the licensed victuallers at any rate that they would like, like other people, a rest on Sunday." Then why do they net have that rest? If I am not misinformed the law does not compel them to keep their shops open on the Sabbath day. At any rate an experiment in Sunday closing was tried by the late Mr. Peter Walker, and neither the magistrates nor the public interfered with his action. Indeed I under- stand that one of the houses that b,longed to him in Warrington still closes its doors on Sundays. But even suppesing there were some legal difficulties about shutting on the seventh day the publican who really desired to enjoy the appointed rest could easily ob- tain a six days license and thus secure his object. Again, Mr. Tarbuck says, " The Bible allowed them to eat on the Sabbath day, and if they had something to eat they would want ecienething to drink." Quite true. And our gracious Creator has supple I US with an abundance both of food and drink—food in the kindly fruits of the earth, and drink in pure and refreshing water. But it is painfully evident from his suggestion about the time for opening public houses on &today that it was intoxicating liquor and not water that the good man had in his mind. This is deplorible. If the Taro- vision dealer can supply all our food wants in six days of the weak and rest on the seventh, and we have an abundance of water why should the publican be called upon to forego any portion of the Sabbath to minister to appetities for drinks that are not required at all, and are in themselves destructive to the minds and bodies of men. And lastly, in his discourse the Vicar told his disciples that" they were conamittine a great sin if they bought a pennyworth of sweets on Sunday, a cigar, or an ounce of tobacco, and ha would prefer them making these purchases as late as twelve o'cloek on Saturday night (alas, for the confectioner and tobeceonist) rather than do anything to encourage Sunday trading." Placing this remarkable dictum alongside hid suggestion about the opening of the public houses One is ana)zed at his reculiar logic. If it Le a great sin to sell and buy a few sweets or a bit of tobacco—things comparatively harm- less—on a Sunday, how much greater must be the sin of those who vend and consume en that day liquors which sannot be produced without violating the Sabbath, and wheel fruits are drunkenness, pauperism, and crime. Yours truly, lONA.

PARISH COUNCIL MENTING.—A neee'ing of this council was held on Monday evening in the eeho droom. There were present: Mr J C Parr (chairman) and Messr.. Lister-Kaye, Jenkins, Chamberlain, Long, Boere- man, Bradford, Warburton, Ellison and Limas, with Woodcect (clerk).—The CI, rk reported that a committee consisting of Councillors Long and Lister-Kaye:along with himself (Mr. Woodcock), met Mr. Swinton on May 18th re the vil,age street im- provements. The Surveyor proposed to lay 10 inch eurb3 along the footpath for the remaining portion and it was decided that this portion be paved w.th small paving stones and that portion where cures are already down to be made good work with gravel or limestone clippings from Grappenhall Bridge to the flat portion of the road, about 60 yards to be macadam with remaining narrow part level with the church wall to pave with square grey setts and the places to be made good by repairing and placing some material between to fill up the hollow spaces.—Mr. Jenkins 'moved that " the Clerk write to the Chairman of the main roads and bridges Committee of the County Council to the effect that we as a Parish Council deplore the bad state of the footpath by the Dog and Dart, carved by the dragging up of the said foot. path by the Warrington Corporation to lay their water main, and we think the Surveyor should not have paved the work in such an incomplete state."—lr. Chamber- lain seconded the resolution and it was carried.—The Clerk said every seven years the Council formed a fresh basis for making the county rate, and he had received a copy of a circular giving particulars of the new basis for the different areas for the County Council rate. The increase in the basis for their rateable value was about £l,OOO, and in several townships, particularly Warbur- ton, the new basis was less than the rateable value. In reply to Mr. Long, Me Woodcock said it was £350 of an increase on this occasion, but it might be £l,OOO on the whole, i.e , the eaves of the basis over the rateable value. —Mr. Jenkins: Does it not show how it is cauaed?— The Clerk said it showed nothing whatever.—Mr. Lone: That is, competing it with Abe new poor rev assess- ment.—The Clerk : Yes ; but we have to collect on it. Mr. Jenkins moved that "the Clerk be ins!reictcd to write concerning a piece of land situated near the locks which belonged to the parish and which by passing out of their hands, the parish sustained an annual loss co' 22s.—The Chairman said the Highway Board had the charge of it and he presumed the Board being merged into; the Rural District Council, the Rural District had the disposition of their money. —Mr. Jen- kins said it was parish propertvand he thought those who had charge of it originally ought not to have allowed it to have gone over.—Mr. Long said in these days the Highway Board had the benefit of it.—After some disco sion it was decided that the Clark should write to the Clerk of the Rural District Council to know what had become of the money.—This was all the business of importance.

THE FATALITY IN A ST. HELENS. COLLIERY.

INSUFFICI EN r PROPPING.

On Monday forenoon MrdS. Brighouse,county coroner resumed an inquest at the Griffin Hotel, Peasley Cross, St. Helens, on the body of Francis Needoam, 47 rears of age, of Frederick-street, who was killed by a fall of coal at White Cros3 Colliery on Friday morning last. Mr. D. F H Matthew 4, Government inspectoriof Mines, Mr. J. Butler, miners' agent, and Mr. J. Pattison, certificated manager of the colliery,were present.—The first witness called was John Grundy, a collier, who said that he and mother man named Burke were working with Need. ham in the main delph mine of the colliery, about midnight on 'thursday. Witness was getting the coal, and deceased was filling the waggons. When the acci- dent happened they had finished fgetting the " bottoms" out, and were preparing to let the "tops" down. They did not put any timber under the top coal as they went along. Thee were about 7ft. under the top coal when the accident happened. They had known "top' coal to be mined under for hundreds of yards' A " gulch " suddenly came which blew ont their lights and the coal came down. Needham was buried underneath. There was plenty of timber close by for propping the coal.— In reply to Mr. Matthews, witness said they did not notice th4t the coal had yielded a bit while they wne at work.—William Burnett, foreman, said he had occupied ' that post for four years. He examined the place three hours before the acc'clent happened, and the '• tops " seemed quite solid and safe. He expected the men to go on getting more bottom coal as long as they dared so as ti make the tops easier to come down.—The Coroner i said it seemed that the men went on with the bottom ; coal as long as they dared. Experienced men would have put props under if they found the roof giving way at al. In answer to queitions, witness said that some of the old miners had been in toe place longer than ha had, and should .know when to prop the top coal—The Coroner : That is where the weakness Comes in. A man is appointed who does not know as much as those unoer him, and leaves it to them, instead of taking the respousibility on himself.—Mr. Matthews . He puts too much confidence in the men. If you had a system of propping every sft. this would frit have happened.—Witness : No.—Mr. Mattbiews said there appeared to be much carelessness is leaving the top coal unsupported in that manner. It should either have been prepped or taken down.—The Coroner, in summing up, said it seemed as if the men had been left to do as they liked, and suggested that they should be visited oftener by th? foreman. The jury knew that much discussion was go.ing. on at. present in regard to the question of systematic timbering, and it would be much better if colliers were not allowed to do as they liked. The jury returned a verdict of "&ccidental death," and added a presentment to the effect that they were of opinion that the foreman had not taken the necessary precautions for the safety of the men.—The Coroner said he quite agreed with that expression of opinion. The colliers should be protected against themselves, and the foreman ought to have exercised the rights attaching to his position.

ST. HELENS.

THE SUICIDE OF A ST. HELENS COLLIE. On Saturday Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest at St. Helens Town Hall, on the body of Jonathan Finney, a collier, of 15, Mossbank•street, Parr, who was found dreweed in the canal at Pocket Nook, on Friday morning. It appeared that deceased had been employed at Ashton's Green Colliery, and on Tuesday night he complained to a fellow collier named Pickavance that he had a pain in his side. He was" jumping about in agony," Pickavance said. Witness went up the shaft with him early on Wednesday mornins,,,and Finney who seemed to be a hit queer, started to go in the direction of his home. Other evidence showed Finney did not return home, but about three o'clock in the morning Police-conetable 11cMike.n saw him running to- wards Pocket Nook. As nothing further was heard of him dragging operations were carried out en Wednesday Tbursday, and Friday morning, when his body was pulled to the side. He appeased to have stuck head first In the mud at the bottom of the canal. In reply to a question, Pickavance said that when they were coming up the, shaft Finnsy attempted to jump out of the cage. The jury returned a verdict of " Suicide whilst of un- sound mind." AN EXPLOSION SCARE AT ST. HELENS. During the greater part of Tuesaay and Wednesday, the inhabitants of Peas.ley Cross and that part of St. Helens when the recent disastrous explosion occurred were alarmed by loud reports, which seemed to indicate that a number of explosions were taking place in the neighbourhood. The school children at Peesley Cross, who have been in a state of panic since their schools were shattered by the explosion were in a state of terror on Tuesday. The noise went on throughout the whole of Wednesday. The matter was referred to at the meeting of the Health Committee on Wednesday afternoon bv Councillor Burehall, who asked what the explosions were, He suggested that something should be done so stop them, as they were causing considerable alarm. Alderman Foster (chairman) explained that the white Cross Colliery Company had a number of old rail- way waggon wheels, which they were breaking by moans of roburite. He thought that Councillor Hough- ton had explained the cause of the explosions to the People of Pe,asley Cross, and he thought that very soon the whole town would be aware that there was nothing to:fear from these explosions.
