A WAR SCARE AND ITS SEQUEL.

During one of the many war scares in which we have indulged any time these twenty years, a couple of her Majesty's gunb,ats were watching the Australian coast, or rather watching any suspicious craft in those waters. As is often the caise along that coast, they had met with dreadful weather, and had been buffeted about and their progress greatly delayed, so by the date the harbour I speak of was reached ample time had elapsed for war to be declared ,and it had seemed imminent enough a week before, when the ships had left their last port of call. Now this harblur held a sort of inner harbour which I would have been very convenient to an enemy for coal- hie, and where in fact large stores of coal were kept on board hulks. So it was quite on the cards that if war had broken out during those few blank days, the enemy might have made a pounce for the coal, more especially as in those days the harbeur was absolutely undefended. Now I atai told, it bristles with big guns ;it was la.ze of a full- moon night when these vessels crept quietly into the gaiter harbour. All looked peaceful enough, and the light from the lighthouse shone out as usual. It did not take leng to decide that a small armed party had better pay a surprise visit to that lighthouse, and learn what had taken place during the last week or so in its neighbourhood. The young officer who told me the story, described most amusingly the precautions taken to avoid any noise, and to surround the lighthouse whilst he and some °there went in to see what was to be found inside. Only one solitary man met them, how- ever, who stood up and saluted stolidly, but off.red no shadow of resistance, and all seemed en regle. The next , thing naturally, was to question this lighthouse keeper, hut to every demand he only shook his head. Tne sock of foreign laeguages which had accompanied that expedition was but small, however, and a shake of the head was the only answer to the same questions repeated in French aid German. It was therefore decided to t Ike the silent man back to the gunhost (leaving a couple of men in char !e of the light). an', see whether, as my informant said, they could " raise any other lingo" on board. But by the time the ship was reachel the doctor and re,t the schoolmaster was rrquired, far the p,or man was found to be in an epilen- tie fit. Daylieht brought a little shore boat alongside with his wife in it, who gave them all a very disagree- able quarter of an hour, for the lighthouse keeper was deaf and dumb, and could not imagine what crime he had committed to be tiken prisoner in that summary fashion. He knew nothing of wars or rumours of wars, but tended his lamps carefully, and his wife had been allowed,'under the elrcumstaneea, to share his solitude. —From " Colonial ..ltemol'ie3," in the " Cornhill Maga- zine " for July.

OUR LOCAL CHURCELES.

EMMANUEL REFORMED CHURCH-

On Sunday, the Sunday School Anniversary services were held in this church the preacher being the Right R:y. Bishop Eldridge, Presiding Bishop of the Refermed Episcopal Church in England. The special hymns pre- pared for the occasion by the Sunday scholars and choir were efficiently sung and they added much interest to the proceedings of the day. At the morning service the Bishop took for his text Isa. 11. 6. " A little child shall lead them," in the evening he preached from the words "The children which God hath graciously given thy servant." Gen. 33. 5. Both sermons were appropriate to the occasion, that in the morning inaicated the great importance of child life and the powerful influence exercised by the purity and inno- cence of children. The consequent duty of carefully training the young in the fear ot the Lord was earnestly, enforced. The discourse in the evening, which was ad- dressed especially to parents and teachers, contained much sound and practical advice as to parental respon- sibility. In the afternoon an impressive confirmation service was held,at which 16 candidates were confirmed. The first address of the Bishop to the candidates pointed out that the rite of confirmation woe of no avail unless it was preceded by repentance toward God and faith towards our Lord Jesus Christ; In his second address the necessity for the individual acceptance of the Holy Spirit was pressed home upon the confirmers. At the close of the evening service, the whole of those who had been confirmed, to- gether with a large number of other communicants par- took of the Lord's Supper. The attendarres throughout the day were satisfactory. Collections were taken up in aid of the Sunday school. A STARTLING PROPHECY.

Mr. George Waite, of Uphoilaed, lec':ured in the Christadephian Room, Bold-street, on Sunday, on" startling prophecy on the Word of God, relating to great and important changes in these latter days." The lecturer identified present day powers with those named in the prophecy in question (Ezekiel 33) and showed that the two opposing powers were Russia and England with their allies. England was in Egypt at the present time, preparatory to the conflict foreshown in the prophecy under consideration, whieh positiop students of prophecy pointed out many years ago would have to be taken by Egypt, because God had said it should be so in His Word. According to that Word, England would be thrown out of England,and the matter was of present and pressing interest on account of other matters which were occurring, which showed that the event was not far hence. Mr. Waite after referring to the Papacy, and showing its identity with various prophecies, pointed te the Zionist movement as a further indication of the near approach of the prophetic epoch under con- sideration. The very dry bones of Israel, among which there was a noise and a shaking, and a coming of bone to his tone, would become "an exceeding great army" and though it would be bad in the coming time o f trouble for England, it would be much worse for Russia the oppressor of Israel. The stone which Daniel saw smite the image would fill the whole earth, 'and unto them that look for him shall Christ appear the second time without sin unto salvation."

RUNCORN SHORTHAND WRITERS' ASSOCIATION.

ANNUL MEETING.

The annual general meeting of the nssc&ation was I held on Monday, at the Association Rooms, Savage's I Bridge. There were present :—Messrs W E Dudley (chairman of the committee), J G Millington, J Simkia, A Dodd, Millington, Senior, R H Bridges, Jones, R Ben- nion (hon. treasurer), A Edwardson, (hon. librarian), C Tomkins (hon. secretary), and C Clare (assistant secretary). In their rep -irt, the committee congratulated the mem- bers upon having emerged successfully through their first session. The Association was formally constituted on November 14th, but owing to delay in finding a suit- able room, it was not possible to begin actual work until quite a month later. The organisation of a Shorthand Writers' Association was entirely new work to the committee, and commencing rather late in the year, it was impossible fer them to arrange anything more than a programme of modest pretentions. Shorthand practice engaged the attention of the members for the greater part of the ses.ion, and at the last few meetings discussions on outlines with blackboard demonstration proved bath interesting and instructive. The committee hoped that would be made a prominect feature of the Association's programme, in the future. During the session lectures were given on the subjects of " An ideal association " obtained from the lecture exchange of the National Federatioe ot S.W. A.'s.) ; " The typewriter and it; uses," by Mr. F.W. Thomasson, member of the Council of the National Federation ; and " The Solar System " (illustrated) by Mr. J. F. Moulton (Runcorn), one of the society's vice- preeidents. On March the 6th an examination was held in connection with the National Federation at at which six candidates were presented. The speeds ranged from 60 te 120 words per minute, the examination being based on a five minutes test. It was evident that the experience was new to the majority of the candidates, as only one member pulled through, viz : Mr. A. Ed wardeon, at the rate of 100 words per minute. The committee hoped, with more practice, to see better results in the future. A second examination was held on June 12th, the results of which were not yet to hand. The report recorded deep regret at the removal by death of Mr. P. Speakman J.P., CA., and Mr. T. A. Milliner_ Mr. Speakman was one of the first to accept the office of vice-president and to wi4l the movement success. Mr. Millinger was a member of the committee and by his death his colleagues felt they bad lost a warm supporter." Mr. R. Bennion presented the balance sheet from which it appeared there was a balance of £2 103 in hand On the motion of Mr. Dodd, seconded by Mr Simkin the report and balance sheet were adopted. On the motion of Mr. W. E. Dudley, seconded by Mr. A. Dodd the following resolution was unanimously adopted :—" That this association do tender a vote of condolence to the wife and family ef the late Mr. Philip Speakman, J.F., C.A., and the parents of the late Mr. T. A. Milliner in the great loss they have severally sustained and begs to express its deep regret at the removal of a worthy vice-president, and an ac- tive member of the committee."

The offic?rg_for the ensuing year were elected ag fol.

lows :—President, Mr Sans-uel Taylor, J.P., Rydal Mount ; vic,-presidents, Messrs James Handley, J.P., F A Lake, J Wilson, J E Meredith ; committee, Messrs W E Dudley, A Dodd, J G Millington, T Clare, Senior, J Simkin. FRODSHAM.

THE SUICIDE IN THE WEAVER CANAL. INQUEST AND VERDICT.

On Friday afternoon Mr. J. C. Bate, coroner held an inquest at the Frodsham Police Station touching the death of Samuel Burkill, labourer, who was found drowned in the Weaver Canal on Thursday morning last week.—Martha Ann Booth stated that she was the wife of William Booth, and resided at Bridge-lane, Frod- sham. Deceased was her father,and lv lived with them, ancliwas a general labourer at Messrs. Ashworth's works. He was 47 years of age. He left home on Wednesday night about twenty minutes past five. He was then going fon night duty at the works. She never saw him alive again. The next moraing whea she got uo at seven o'clock she found the deceased had been in the house and left his watch and chain and basket there. She felt upset as her father was not in the house, but thinking he might have gone to ber sister's she went out to look for him. He had been more or less depressed since his wife's death four year's ago. He never told witness that he would take his life. He had not been off work at all. He was quite comfortable with witness and her husband. Some few weeks ago deceased attended the funeral of an old friend.—Eliza Charles- worth said she resided at the Cave, Frodshaen. She was talking to deceased on Thursday last. She knew he had been rather depressed in his mind, and she asked him if he was any better. She knew he had been suffering from influenza. The deceased replied that he " wished he was buried."—George Rogers, in the employ of Messrs. Ashworth, said he knew deceased very well. He saw him on Thursday morning at the works at about 5-45. He knew he had been ill, and he inquired how he was. Deceased replied that he was worse. Ha had threatened to commit su cide in witness's presence. On Thursday morning deceased told witness it would not be long before he was at Overton (i.e. the church- yard).—Joseph Caldwell stated that on Thursday morning he heard Burkhill was missing, and he went along the Weaver Canal with a man named Rou,ghsedge to see if they could find any trace of the deceased. Oa the canel side they found his jacket and cap. They sent word to the police, and then procured a boat hook and com- menced to drag the canal. P.C. Price came on the scene, and grappling was resorted to with :he assistance of several others, and the body was recovered.—P.C. Price stationed at Frodsham, gave corroborative evidence. He found the last witness dragging with a boat honk. Wit- ness and Mr. Soden got in a boat and searched the canal, and eventually Mr. Boden recovered the body. The legs were tied together with the piece of rope produced.— The jury returned a verdict that the man committed suicide whilst temporarily insane. . .

THE BLURCIAT SCHOOL TREAT TO OVERTON HILLS.— On Wednesday, the boys and girls attending the Blue- coat School, Liverpool, to the number of 330, were enjoying themselves upon the delightful Overton Hills. After thoroughly enjoying themselves, the company returned home from Frodsham by the 9 p in. train. The school was accompanied by its own band, and sweet music was discoursed at intervals. The thunderstorm which passed over the district between five and six forced the company to the shelter of the Mersey View Pavilion, but owing to the magnificent accommodation there, sport was little interfered with.

SCENE IN THE BELGIA.N CHAaIBER OF DEPUTIES. FISTS FREELY USED.

An unprecedented " scene," arising from the agitation ageing', the Government Suffrage Bill, took place in the Belgian Chamber of Representatives on Wednesday. From the opening of the sitting, the Socialists prevented all business by continuous singing, shouting, and whist- ling. The uproar cuhuinated in a rush on the Minis- terial bench. The Government supporters massed round their leaders, and fists were used freely on both sides. Many members pulled others down and rolled on the fixir. The ugliest onslaught was towards the Prime Minister himself, who was reviled as responsible for the Suffrage Bill, but his friends were able to prstct him from serious injory. It was the most violent scene ever enacted in the Belgian Parliament.

DISASTROUS IILL FIRE.

About 2.30 a.m. yesterday morning the inhabitants of Kingswood were alarmed by the cry of" fire" from one of the mills owned by Messrs. Tubbs and Lewis, elastic and silk weavers. Tne fire commenced in the rubber room just over the water wheel, and so rapidly did the flames spread that within an hour the mill was destroyed with the exception of the walls and a chimney stack. Messrs Tubbs and Lewis employ over 600 people and as the other two mills are dependent upon the one destroyed for the supply of elastic, it is feared that 200 persons, chiefly women and girls, will be thrown out of employment. The damage is estimated at £50,000 which is covered by insurance. WIDNES THEATRE STRUCK BY LIGHTNING.

On Wednesday evening during the heavy thunder- storm which passed over Widnes the Alexandra Theatre was struck by lightning. A chimney stack was de- molished, but fortunately the main body of the spaciou building was uninjered, the debris falling on the land adjacent. The people working in the theatre were greatly alarmed at the occurrence. DROWNING FATALLTY IN THE THAMES.

At six o'clock yesterday morning, on the Thames, a barge collided with Kew Bridge. A lighterman,a woman named ficdtord, and a gin i of 12,were swept off the deck by the tiller,which was swung round by the rush of the tide. The woman and the girl were drowned and the barge sank. MORE THUNDERSTORMS. FIVE HEIFERS KILLED.

During au exceptionally severe thunderstorm which pamed over Market Drayton and neighbourhood on Wednesday night many houses in the town were flooded tor a considerable deptb. At Sowdley, an adjacent village, five valuable heifers belonging to Mr. Beeston were killed. NARROW ESCAPE OF RACEHORSES.

The Duke of Westminster's famous horse Orme and a valuable colt named Wail, by Bond Or—Gluntlet, had a narrow escape during Wednesday's terrific storm. The animals were in adjoining stalls in the yearling paddock at Eaton. The lightning struck the roof of the building, stripping off it about a cartload of slates, and splitting some ornamental tile work in two. Fortunately neither of the animals was injur.A. A valuable brood mars at Churton, a few miles away, was killed by lightning. YESTERDAY'S CRICKET. THE THIRD TEST MA MB. AN EVEN DAY'S PLAY.

Tee third t^st match was entered upon at Headingley, Leeds, yesterday, in the presence of 20,000 spectatcri. F. S. Jackson and J. Briggs filled the two vacancies in the English team. The Australians won the toss and began batting on a wicket which suffered from the effects of the over-night rain. They made a bad atand, Noble, Belly, and Gregory going out for "ducks," but some grand batting by Worrall and Hill improved matters, the venture ultimately closing for 172, of which total Worrall made 76, and Hill 34. CAN WIRELESS TELEGRAPEUC MESSAGES BE INTERCEPTED?

As much discussien has been provoked by the possibi- lity of wireless telegraphic messages being intercepted during their flight through the air by persons other than for which they were intended, the article in the current Windsor upon " Signor Marconi and Wireless Telegraphy," will be read with timely interest. Regard- ing this point, Mr. Cleveland Moffet, the writer of the article, says : "In the first place it is evident that generals and admirals, as well as privet inoividuals, could always protect themselves by sending their des- patches in cipher. Then, during active military operations, despatches could often be kept within a friendly radius by lewering the wire on the mast until its transmitting power came within that radius. Mar- coni realises, of cours?, the desirability of being able in certain cases to transmit messages in one and orny one direction. To this end he has conducted a special series of experiments with different sending apparatus from that already described. He uses no wee here but a Righi oscillator placed at the focus of a parabolic copper reflector two or three feet in diameter. The waves sent out by this oscillator are quite different from the others, bring only about two feet long instead of three or four hundred feet, and the results up to the present, are less important than those obtained with the pendent wire. Still, in trials on the Salisbury Plain, Marconi and his assistants sent messages perfectly in this way over a distance or a mile and three-quarters, and were able to direct these messages at will by aiming tbe reflector in one direction or another. It appears these Hertzian waves, though invisible, may be concen- trated by parabolic reflectors into parallel beams and projected in narrow lines just as a bull's-eye lantern projects beams of light. And it was found that a very slight shifting of the reflector would step the messages at the receiving end, In other words unless the Elertzian beams fell directly n the receiver there was an end of all communication. Besides the possibility of directing the waves with reflectors, Marconi is now engaged in most promisipg experiments in syntony, which I may describe as the electrical tuning of a particular transmitter to a particular receiver, so that the latter will respond to the former, and no other. That, of course, is a possibility in the future, but it bids fair so- nto be realised. There are even some who main- tain that there may be produced as many separate sets of tranemitters and receivers only capable of working together!us there are separate sets of Yale locks and keys. In that event, any two private individuals might com- municate freely without fear of being misunderstood by 'others,'

NOTES ON GAMES AD ATHLETICS. CRICKET. The Warrington representatives at Arpley were doubly' at home on Saturday—both elevens were without en engagement—so an interesting game was arranged: the first eleven were pitted against the next sixteen. What- ever latent talent there may be amongst the junior members the opportunity was presented of displaying the same, and the result will be that certain members will be given a trial when vacancies occur in the first team. The highest scorer was B. Silcock with 26 runs arid his display was cnly what one expected from his batting last season. It fell to the lot of Dr. Smith to lower hie peg. The batting of both A. Duckworth and E. Pier point was clean, though the former was got out some- what easily. To the credit of tht latter it must be said his dismis•al was the result of a brilli int catch in the long field. Steadiness characterised the batting of R. Crompton and J. Thompson, the latter, we may add, making the second top score for his team. The last five wickets all fell fJr unassumine " ducks," the fast de- liveries of Connor and the wily slows from Podmore proving equally fatal to their hopes and their batting. R. Pierpoint and G. Clarke commenced the essay for the seniors. The first-named fell an early victim to J. Thompson being bowled off his pads. T. Knowles joined Clarke, and despite the number of fielders the batsmen contrived to place the ball safely, runs coming merrily. Changes were frequent in the attack, and it was not until the score had reached 55, before elawtin, who relieved E Pierpoint got Knowles caught at mid-on for a capital 28. With the assistance of E. C. E. Kendle the score was taken to 91, when the last comer's wicket was taken. J. W. Bibby and Dr. Smith were quite off form, but E. Connor stayed with Clarke until the close,the latter carrying out his bat for a splendidly played 59,in which some hard hitting had been done. E. Connor's score of 15 (not out) represented as many min- utes' play. A feature of the play was Kendle's wicket- keeping,which was exceedingly smart. the result of the game was an easy win for the first eleven by 46 runs for five wickets. --- Walker played a very useful game for the Cheshire Lines Engineers on Saturday when they defeated St. Elphin's on their own ground. His batting totalled 32 rurs. Robinson for 16, and John Genders for 11, also rendered able assistance. But for Norman's steady batting the Saints would have been in a much werse --- ' The Cheshire Lines Engineers 2nd eleven were some 30;runs.to the good on their game with Glazebrook on the ground of the latter. The Sankey Wire Mills eleven administered a decided defeat on Widnes White Rose. Hill was prominent in the batting for the latter. Evans and Clarke for tbe Wiremen batted in fine style for their respective scores. Simpson and Kenwright also howled well, the former taking five wickets at a cost of 17 runs and the latter four for 14. -- nSt. Ann's just managed to beat the Friars' green and Brick-street B.S. eleven. Jone s batted well far the latter, and Armitage, sent., and Dutton—who made 15 and 10 respectively—for the latter. ----- The Workingmen's Mission Second Eleven had the best of a game with Fairfield, defeating the latter by 11. runs. For Fairfield Steel made 21 in good style, and Fielding 16. Woolley for the visitors, credited himself with 13. At Runcorn, on Saturday, the Runcorn Juniors met St. Michael's on the ground of the former, the result going to the Juniors by 26 rune. For the latter J. Jolley' was prominent with good batting, compiling 27. Rowleil made a creditable display for St. Mienael'e. Jolley wa9 also in good form with the ball. The Workingmen's Miss- ion accounted for Arpley on Saturday, the former compiling 41. runs, and the latter replying with 17. Harding's 9 for the Mission was the highest score during the game. The result of the game between St. Paul's and Rylands Bros' stiff was a win for the latter. Mason and Bate played well for the Saints and raven and Sanders were prominent among the winners. Elevens from the Warrington and Witton Grammar Sohools met on Saturday the result being in favour of the locals. H. Molyneux batted well for Witton,who found Hesketh'i bowling destructive. J E Bryant and Pier- point made the top scores for the winners.
